1

“Kill him, kill him, kill him!” I screamed.

Special Agent Laura Scharf aimed at the troll and dispatched him with a perfect shot between the eyes, then turned to the next grotesque creature with floppy ears like mine. She shot him too.

“Are we going to do this all night?” Jack drawled from behind us.

A cacophony of buzzing bells erupted, and the screen in front of us dissolved into the words, “High Score.”

“Yes!” I clapped Scharf on the back but stepped away quickly at her raised eyebrow. “Good job?”

“Not bad.” She eyed the arcade game Trolls and Goblins and holstered the blue plastic gun. “More fun with this.” She patted the bulge beneath her peacoat where a real Glock rested in the shoulder holster she wore off duty. Or at least as off-duty as an FBI agent gets.

“If you use that,” I said, patting the arm she didn’t need to draw her weapon, “you would kill the nice arcade game that didn’t do anything to you.” I turned away once she’d entered her initials at the top of the screen. “Thanks for coming.”

“I didn’t know you were such a hockey fan.” Scharf offered a small, tight smile as she scanned the concourse full of milling people in white, dark blue, and red fueling up between hockey innings. She looked like she was scanning for suspects. She stuck out like, well, an FBI agent in a sea of hockey fans.

“Love it,” I said. As she did another full pivot, I couldn’t resist adding, “No dead body is going to jump out of the popcorn.”

“You never know.” She tugged at her peacoat to fit better over her weapon.

Jack wove his fingers with mine and said, “Glad to see y’all get some free time.” He put his lips to my ear as we headed towards our box around a bustling concession stand that reeked of burnt butter. “It would’ve been more fun without the third wheel.”

He had to lean quite a way. He topped my five feet by at least another foot.

I kissed him in reply, but unpeeled quickly, mindful of our third wheel. And her gun. This night was way more awkward than I had expected; before this, Scharf and I spent the two months we’d known each other chasing a murderer and trying not to die.

It hadn’t left many opportunities for small talk. The aforementioned murderer had also derailed my fledgling relationship with Jack, but to my surprise, it was sort of turning into something.

As a mostly unemployed, mostly homeless college dropout, I didn’t think Jack and I were in the same orbit, but in the world of magic, the heir of a goblin king was a perfect match for a demon.

If only my puny human half believed that. I had dragged an FBI agent on a date with him. We still had a few more trust falls in our future before we could claim couplehood. Scharf’s years in the army and then the FBI had made her bad-guy detector way more accurate than mine. I wanted to see what she thought of him.

A rumble of thunder almost shook the building, and all of us turned towards the floor-to-ceiling windows that flanked the concourse. The entrances to the private boxes were on the upper deck, at least three stories up. Out the windows, we could see the roiling sky over the old brownstones of Chinatown.

“Let’s hope that holds off,” Scharf murmured before examining the sign above the hallway leading to the suites, then squinting at the pristine ticket she dug out of a pocket. I had no clue where mine went. The security guard nodded at us as we went through the door.

“Don’t worry, agent,” Jack said. “They’ve already memorized our faces.”

I led them quickly towards our suite, resisting the urge to tell him not to tease her. Wasn’t this exactly what I asked for?

We trooped through real wood double doors into a warm suite full of free drinks and plush maroon seats facing the ice far below. The lighting was low and soothing, and the walls were a neutral beige. Yes, even in uber-fancy sporting arenas, they still went for DC beige. We had a panoramic view of the cavernous arena and three tiers of seats that stretched up to the rafters.

Jack was a chef at a swanky DC restaurant, and a regular had offered him the tickets. The dude had invited about ten people to this shindig. The rest were all lawyers like him. I felt spectacularly underdressed in a crinkly red dress I’d snagged at a thrift store over jeans. It was February. I’d thought it had looked fantastic on the rack, but I struggled not to feel tacky next to a fortune in custom-tailored suits and sports gear.

Scharf and I made for our seats in the back row as Jack mingled, clearly at home with the crowd. He wore a jersey with the number two on it and dark jeans that fit him like a glove. The red letters on the jersey matched his red hair and his freckles, which he had all over. All over. There were parts of Jack my human half was not worried about at all.

One lawyer said, “Twins!” when he noticed he and Jack wore the same player’s jersey. Jack guffawed and cuffed him across the shoulders.

Scharf settled in the third seat in the row, though she looked at the aisle with longing as I sat next to her, folding my legs beneath me and relaxing. These seats were clearly built for sedentary executives, not FBI agents whittled by permanent running addictions or five-foot-nothing goblins.

“This is fun, right?” I asked as she scanned the lawyers as diligently as the fans on the concourse.

“The team is playing like they’ve already run a marathon. They’re exhausted.”

“So not a good game?” I asked, unable to think of anything intelligently hockey-like to say as the lights went down and something classic rock started pumping through the arena. The crowd seemed unmoved. DC does not pump it up.

Jack plopped down beside me and gave me another kiss.

“Hi,” he said with a grin. I reveled for a second in the shiver it sent down my spine. He spent a second just looking at me with his other-worldly blue eyes like I was the only other person in the entire arena. Whatever else was between us, his undivided attention was completely addicting. “Have I told you yet you look beautiful in red?”

I rolled my eyes and hid my grin. “Twice.”

Until a few weeks ago, I would never have dreamed of wearing red, because my skin was orange. Literally. My skin tone came from my goblin father. Some people have a winter skin tone, some a summer. On my best days, my skin tint looked like I’d poured the cheapest spray tan on the shelf over every inch of me. But one new way I’d figured out to use my magic was adjusting my appearance with a glamour over the real me. I’d chosen a paler, less neon skin color and tamer blond hair that looked fantastic in red.

People say be yourself, but those people don’t have magical glamour to be a better yourself.

Another lawyer passed by, and Jack pivoted to shake his hand like they were trying to break each other’s arms before settling back, his arm casually around my shoulders.

When the teams didn’t emerge, I asked, “When is intermission over?”

He burst out laughing, attracting lawyerly attention that had me fighting not to shrink in the plush seat. “You’re such a huge fan?”

I tried to think what had given me away; faking sports knowledge usually requires cheering when everyone else does. “Not an intermission?”

“No.” He half-raised out of his seat to shake and smash another dude on the back as he passed our seats; they’d met before. They’d all met before.

He also waved at a pimply teenager who passed him trying to keep a boat of nachos from tipping over. The kid wore a jersey three sizes too big and an expression that morphed between childlike exuberance when he looked at the ice and teenage boredom whenever his father glanced at him.

When they sat in front of us, Jack’s eyes returned to mine. “Confess.”

I told the room not to notice me and said, “Okay, no, but Scharf likes sports, and I wanted you guys to talk when we weren’t, you know, about to get killed.”

I whispered the last word even though no one could hear me. I couldn’t really disappear, but I could convince people to ignore me. Goblins are champion cowards and much of my magic, like unnoticing and glamour, was dedicated to hiding and running away.

He pulled me into him with an arm around my shoulders. “You’re a pippin.”

“A what?”

“A pippin? It’s an apple tart. It’s a phrase from…” The players finally burst out of the locker room, and he surged to his feet without finishing that thought. I wondered when I would get the courage to ask him how old he was. When you stop taking FBI agents on dates, maybe?

He settled into his seat again and said, “Okay, you’re a good egg.”

“Nobody says that but Scharf. But thanks.”

When our team won the tussle at center ice, the teen one row ahead of us stood up, and his nachos tipped off his lap. Before they hit the ground, I danced a couple of queso-dipped chips into my hand using my magic. Another part of my magic was the ability to manipulate earth, or anything made or grown from it. I popped the first chip into my mouth and grinned at the chemical salt flavor.

Scharf said, “Stop that.”

“Aww.” I quickly chomped down the second chip.

“Number one: it’s stealing. Number two: stop that. I liked it better when you couldn’t access your magic without a bargain.”

“So you could control when I use it?”

“So you could only use it when you needed it. No stealing food from kids!”

Until a few weeks ago, I had to bargain with someone to gain power, always having to give up something to gain anything. I was a little embarrassed at how long it took me to figure out that since I was half-human, I could bargain with myself and do whatever whenever I wanted.

“They were going to end up on the ground anyway,” I couldn’t resist saying.

Jack snapped his fingers, and what looked to be a hot dog drowning in chili landed in my lap.

Scharf froze beside me. That wasn’t a metaphor; every muscle in her body tensed, and she did not move for a solid five seconds. I nudged her, and she deliberately relaxed in her seat, joint by joint. But her eyes never left Jack.

Gingerly, I righted the flimsy cardboard boat before I wore the chili. Called half-smokes, hot dogs were one unqualified delight in DC. The overflowing bucket smelled of spices, tomatoes, and grease. My mouth flooded with saliva.

He frowned at the hot dog. “Sorry. They looked the same to my Sight.”

“This is good. Thanks,” I said, my eyes on Scharf.

I tried for a couple of seconds to get my hands on the blistered, oozing dog, but the chili filled the little boat to the brim with mustard and onions on top. “Uh, fork?”

Jack grinned as he brought my hand to his lips to lick my index finger free of chili. He effortlessly ignored Scharf staring at him as the lights came up and the players faced off in the center again.

I fought a smile, but said firmly, “Fork.”

He snapped his fingers again as the referee dropped the ball, and a rain of white plastic cutlery in little baggies threatened to swamp the bowl.

I unpeeled one and dug into the chili.

“Careful with those, they’re compostable,” Scharf said.

I noticed they were off-white and bumpier than a normal disposable fork. “Why is that bad?”

“They’ll melt in the chili,” she said with a smile.

I stabbed the dog; the fork seemed pokey enough for processed meat.

In front of us, the players took off in what looked like completely random directions. The music gave way to the quiet snick snick of skates on the ice and the asymmetrical rhythm of sticks hitting the ball. I ignored the game as I took my first bite.

The smokey, spicy taste reminded me of a dozen childhood carnivals before my father had disappeared from my life, and the nostalgia was a tiny warm blanket in the frost between my companions and the literal temperature of the arena.

Scharf fingered one of the fork packets that had ended up in her lap, still eyeing Jack, and asked quietly, “Where did this come from?”

“The concession stands. Not a kid. Don’t worry,” Jack said, sneaking an arm around my shoulders again.

“And is there money in the drawer for it?”

He cleared his throat and snapped his fingers again.

She sat back. “You two are perfect for each other.”

“Hey, thanks!” we said at the same time, though her tone was flat, and I couldn’t read if she approved or not.

Scharf mumbled to herself, “Of course, I thought Jennifer and I were the perfect match, too.”

Jennifer was her ex, who’d moved to DC with her from her posting out West somewhere, then broke up with her less than a year later. Nice girl.

Not.

Jack stole my loaded fork from me when I’d gotten the perfect bite of bun, dog, chili, and mustard and popped it into his mouth.

“Mean!”

He kissed me and let me have a taste. I pulled away again just as quickly as before. He came up for air with his eyes on Scharf, apparently no longer able to ignore her FBI death glare.

He kissed me again and got up, banging his knee on his armrest and cursing. When somebody in the front of the box twisted around at the sound, Jack waved a hand, and everyone in the box faced forward with arms on their armrests. Despite two players dashing towards the goal, nobody moved, and nobody clapped.

I was torn between admiring his politeness and fearing his ability to hypnotize humans into puppets. I hoped Scharf hadn’t noticed the trick.

“You guys chat,” he said, looking at Scharf, and disappeared out the doors at the back. The moment they closed; our neighbors shook themselves. One of the breakaway players faked a shot but really passed to his teammate. The guy guarding the goal thing dove for where the ball wasn’t, leaving the net clear for the real shot. Our box erupted in cheers.

Our player missed. The arena groaned. Even I could tell he’d screwed that one up. People in our box slumped in their seats or threw up their hands, and I released the breath I didn’t realize I’d been holding.

“You didn’t tell me he was a goblin,” Scharf said, very casually.

“Who, Jack? He isn’t.”

“But he can snap his fingers for hot dogs.” Sometime in the last minute, she’d become Agent Scharf, gentling her voice to her interrogation calm.

I knew not to be taken in. “I can’t snap for food, and I’m a goblin.”

“Then what is he?”

“That’s beside the point.”

“No, that’s the entire point. What is he?” She asked this like she was asking for the weather tomorrow, her voice silky smooth and quiet. I wished she was yelling.

People around us erupted in anger as the other team got the ball after another center stand-off and broke towards our net. The boy and his father in front of us leaped to their feet, so we both missed what happened as they reached it.

The crowd booed as Scharf tried to see around the dad. I didn’t bother. I couldn’t see or understand enough to know what was upsetting them. But even I could sense the mood had changed; the jovial celebration was gone. Eventually, the boos from the arena petered out and people resumed their seats, but they were watching tensely.

Scharf leaned closer to me, watching the ice and the crowd in a surveillance sweep. “It doesn’t matter what I think of him. The fact that you even asked my opinion means you don’t trust him.”

“I do, too.”

“And you have no sense of self-preservation whatsoever. So I have to ask myself, why don’t you, a powerful magical creature, trust him? What is worse than a goblin?”

“Worse?” I asked, stung.

She winced but plowed on. “More powerful? Scarier? Answer the question, Orange.”

On the ice, we had the ball, and then we didn’t have the ball and the other team did, and it set the crowd booing and standing again.

Scharf was not distracted. “I think the answer to my question is also the answer to your question.”

“I didn’t ask a question.”

“What do I think of Jack? That’s why I’m here, isn’t it?”

“Can you not be an FBI agent for two seconds, please?”

Scharf scrubbed a hand through her short dark hair, leaving it askew. “Sorry.” The soft calm of interrogation was gone. Everyone took their seats, and we watched the players get nowhere for another minute.

“Look, I don’t dislike him,” she said in her normal voice. “I even like him.”

“Are those different?”

“Yes. And he’s crazy about you. And he’s more powerful than you, on a number of levels, and that worries you. And so it worries me.”

I slumped. I never would have thought that her objection would be the fact she thought I had an objection. Well, you do, don’t you?

We watched each team try for the other side without success again. “Orange, have some fun, go to some hockey games. Maybe learn something about hockey first. Enjoy him. Just… I don’t know what. Just be careful.”

“I’m not really…”

“Careful? No, you really are not.” We shot at the net but missed again, and she waited until the cheer/boo cycle petered off before saying, “I’m not going to ask again, but I want to.”

“What is he? I’m not sure, myself,” I lied, keeping the fact that my boyfriend was a demon from my lapsed catholic… Landlady? Crime-fighting partner? Jack wasn’t the only relationship I was struggling to define.

“Also maybe learn more about him,” she added, “And please call me when you stay over there.”

I’d been splitting my time between Jack’s and her couch. I smiled; one word for her was friend. “Deal.”

Too late I heard the chime in my head signaling a magical bargain, meaning whether I liked it or not, I would find myself with a phone in my hand at Jack’s house.

“Damn it, not a magic deal like…” I petered off.

“Deal,” Scharf said firmly and settled in her seat with a smile on her face, truly relaxed for the first time that evening. “I feel better.”

“Good for you.”

Suddenly, for no reason I could see, a guy from our team threw down his stick and started screaming something. Scharf’s relaxation lasted two seconds. The crowd took up the chant. “Offsides. He was offsides!”

The other team kept skating as the crowd started stamping.

One player tripped another player with his stick and suddenly we were watching a boxing match, not a hockey game. At first, it was only two players, but one went down and more flew off both benches to go after the ones on the ice. Someone close to us screamed, “Ref, you blind idiot!”

“Why is this going bad?” Scharf murmured, her right-hand inching across her chest towards the gun.

“It’d be worse if you draw… what’s in your armpit,” I said, batting at her hand.

A player from the opposing team in blue postured in front of the referee, and then he swung his stick, clocking the guy across the head. That silenced the crowd. For a second.

Scharf leaped to her feet, all semblance of civilian enjoying a sporting event disappearing, but we were trapped floors above the ice.

“Is that a legal move?” I asked, attempting to get her back in her seat by tugging on her coattails.

“Assault? Battery? Possibly with a deadly? No, it isn’t!”

Somebody from the opposing team decked that guy, and then the rest of the players piled on, both benches emptying onto the ice. Soon, it was an all-out brawl. The announcer called for calm, but nobody listened.

As the pile on the ice heaved to reveal the ref on the ground with a bloody streak across his head, the crowd roared. They rushed towards the ice, held only by a few sheets of plastic around the rink. It didn’t stop them long.

A crash and a screech of sparks heralded a falling barrier, and angry fans joined the players.

Someone grabbed my left shoulder, and I screamed. The teenager screamed too, leaping away from us, but it was Jack. “Come on!”

As I stood up, Scharf followed, muttering to herself, “Why did this go bad so fast?”

“She’s right.” Jack grabbed my hand and snapped at the dude between us and the door, who froze. We shimmied around the suddenly comatose lawyer.

“What is he?” Scharf asked, peering into the lawyer’s expressionless face.

“You weren’t going to ask,” I shouted as we made it to the double doors.

Jack could hardly open the doors against the crowd outside. Fans with a better sense of self-preservation were already fleeing.

Jack looked at me, squished up against him, and snapped his fingers again. The people behind the doors pivoted and moved in unison away. He got it open and dragged me between the human statues. I whispered, “Don’t compel them!”

“What?” Scharf yelled.

“Never mind!”

The three of us staggered towards the stairs as the arena was lost to the mob.

Jack acted as a battering ram through the crowds, and we reached the concourse in front of the concession stand in record time, but not quickly enough. Hundreds of people crowded the open space, trying to get away, and I couldn’t see the stairs to the ground at all.

Scharf grabbed my free hand. “Don’t let go.”

I swallowed and nodded. Jack grabbed my other hand, and we surged towards the stairs that would take us down and out.

Then Scharf tried to go one way, and Jack went the opposite around a family trying to protect their popcorn buckets, and Jack lost the tug of war on my arm as I tried to decide which way to follow.

Between one moment and the next, he was gone, and I couldn’t turn around because people were crowding us from behind, trying to get through us. I shouted his name and tried to jump, even levitating myself a bit off the ground.

Since I was made mostly of earth, I could lift my body, but it took an insane amount of energy, and I thumped down. No sign of him. I’d looked away for five seconds; it was all it took to lose him. I turned to Scharf. “I can’t find Jack!”

She didn’t hear me. She had her cell phone pressed to her ear and shouted, “Talk to Metro, because a massive, angry, crazy crowd is about to hit the streets. I don’t know why! It doesn’t make any sense... No, I said, ‘any sense!’ Never mind!”

The current carried us along: me trying to find Jack, Scharf trying to keep her phone against her ear, our hands gripped together. I tried for my phone but couldn’t get enough space to get it out of my back pocket.

“I can’t find Jack!” I shouted again.

“He can take care of himself!”

As my heart pounded in the ever-closer quarters, I tried to reassure myself that Scharf and I could take care of ourselves, too. A goblin and an FBI agent weren’t exactly helpless in a crowd of humans, however crazy they were.

We were shoved toward the bottleneck at the stairs, then slowed to a crushing stop. The air grew hotter, louder, and more difficult to breathe.

Scharf said into her phone, “You’re kidding me!”

“What is it?”

“It’s the Lunar New Year.”

“I know?”

“And we’re in the heart of Chinatown.”

“I know! Didn’t you notice the parade when we came in?” A series of dancers and drummers had been walking down the street.

“That was a parade?” she asked.

“Yeah?”

“Well, apparently there are more people out there now,” Scharf said. “The subway is jammed, and the streets are full. Eighteen thousand people are about to pile out of the arena.”

I cringed. “We have to get out of here.”

The sound of shattering glass temporarily silenced the crowd. One of the ten-foot-tall windows had splintered. I couldn’t see what caused it, but a few guys on the balcony of a brownstone across from the window cheered madly. Then somebody wearing a jersey with the number two, the same number Jack wore, went plunging out the window.

That’s all I saw before he disappeared. I reached with magic but couldn’t catch him in time. His fading scream echoed even above the roar of the crowds.

2

“Someone went out the window,” Scharf shouted. “Stop!”

Scharf tried to move against the stream of people to reach the gaping hole, but a huge bald man behind us protested and shoved her hard into me.

Panic weighed my limbs and constricted my chest. “We have to get out of here.”

I smelled cologne, beer, nacho cheese, and sweat. The temperature was climbing, and I fought to get air in my lungs. When you’re the shortest person in the room, an angry crowd gets old fast.

When somebody elbowed me in the kidneys, I screeched and pushed on it with magic.

Scharf shook her phone. “Hello?”

“I don’t think that helps,” I told her.

“No service. Too many people.” She cursed again and put the phone back on her belt.

The bald guy beside me deliberately pushed me away, and I said, “Okay, buddy. That’s it.”

For a second, anger overtook panic, burning my lungs. I tightened my fists, readying to push back at him.

“Orange, get us to the window,” Scharf said quietly, her hand on my fist.

I stretched my fingers, shocked by my sudden anger. Had I just gathered magic to shove an innocent bald guy out of a building? I shook my hands and my head. We had to get out of here.

With the burst of magic I’d summoned, I used my short height to my advantage and started shoving sideways, pulling Scharf along behind me as people reacted to the unexpected direction and let us through.

In less than a minute, we were out of the crush and pinned instead against the intact window next to the broken one, our backs against a floor-to-ceiling banner of a pop star I didn’t recognize mid-concert. Another banner of a tall dude in red with a basketball flapped in the broken window.

I fought it aside to glance down. There was no one splattered on the pavement. I sagged into the casing and jumped away when a jagged piece of industrial glass grazed my biceps.

“I think it was Jack!”

“Who was Jack?”

“Who jumped. Nobody’s dead!”

“What is he?” Scharf glanced at the ceiling. In prayer? Old habit?

“Never mind that. Now we’re farther away from the stairs.”

A drunk dude in the opposing team’s jersey shoved me. A loud crack behind me doubled my heart rate. I had to dig deep to keep him from going out the broken window and me through the other.

I clenched my hands and shoved the crowd and me away from the windows, determined not to let anyone innocent die, wondering whether Jack broke the window on purpose and bailed without me, endangering all these people.

I shook off the thought, not liking my answer. Scharf swept the popstar banner aside to reveal a spider web of cracks exactly Orange shaped.

“We have to get out of here,” Scharf said, preternaturally calm, her eyes on the crack.

I looked at her and then at the open window, blinking against a spray of freezing water.

“You’re made of earth,” I told Scharf to convince her and me. “And you’re much smaller than a car.”

A few weeks ago, I’d lifted Scharf’s sedan when we’d gotten pushed off a bridge. I wiped my sweaty palms on my jeans and ignored my nerves and the voice in my head explaining all the ways this was different.

Don’t screw up. I grabbed around her arms in an awkward hug.

“Orange, what are you doing?”

“Being stairs.”

I told everyone around me to forget I was there. I knew I would go unnoticed, but I still hadn’t tested whether anyone would notice what I was holding. My clothes always disappeared with me, but would another person?

We’d find out. Right now.

I met Scharf’s eyes, which were six inches above mine: dark brown—almost black—showing alarm and confusion, but also trust.

I jumped and called on my magic to get us both out the window.

Scharf instantly put her arms around me. We jostled, and I said, “Don’t move!”

There was a rough moment where I almost tangled us in a string of red lanterns, but I added a little more juice to bob over them. No one screamed and pointed at the floating people, so I guessed I was right, and we were both invisible.

I lowered us toward the ground forty feet below as fast as I could without breaking Scharf. I would not have the juice to do this for long.

Icy rain plastered our hair to our heads and my beautiful dress around my torso. When a pellet of ice hit my forehead, I realized it was worse than rain: a disgusting, dangerous sleet the East Coast weather people called a “wintry mix.”

“Orange, there are people down there,” Scharf said in an aggressively calm voice she used when we were in real trouble.

I was pleased that she was almost impossible to shock because I’d forgotten about the crowds. I opened my eyes and ended up with my head under one of her armpits, my face smashed against her weapon as I attempted to see the ground. Yep. Completely covered with people. Spotting a little slice of the roof, I tried to aim for that. But manipulating the earth in my body had a lot more to do with falling slowly and a lot less to do with flying. We were going down.

Scharf said, “Go visible.”

“What?”

“Visible! Now!”

I let go of the “unnoticing,” and people shrieked.

“Move, move, move!” Scharf shouted. A hole opened below us, and we plopped onto the sidewalk outside the arena into a greasy puddle.

We untangled ourselves as I gulped for air and watched people fleeing the main doors of the arena with bruised and bloody faces.

“Come on,” she said. “Let’s go.”

She took off north, and I shouldered my way through the crowds after her up Seventh Street dodging shouting people, overflowing trash bins, and the detritus from the parade and the game.

We passed under an ornate gate over the road in vivid jewel tones of aquamarine, maroon, indigo, and gold that marked the center of Chinatown and turned right onto a side street full of restaurants. A few were Chinese with their picture menus glowing in their front windows, but there was also an Irish Pub and a few other American chains.

I pointed behind us and said, “Your car’s that way!”

Scharf looked behind us at the crowd rippling away from the arena. “You want to wade through that? I was going to go around the block.”

I nodded. “Better plan.”

I shoved my hands in my pocket, shivering in my wet dress. I had an orange leather jacket that would have been perfect for the weather, but I hadn’t wanted to wear it because I didn’t know what color the opponent had been. I didn’t know much, but I knew you did not wear the wrong color to a game. And it clashed with the dress, which seemed like a minor problem now. The dress was way too flimsy for this storm.

We walked past a place selling Thai bubble tea, a Japanese restaurant that sold sandwiches, and a fusion restaurant selling Thai, sushi, and Chinese food, though all I could smell was the motor oil the rain was blasting off the streets.

Someone whooped behind us, followed by the familiar splinter of shattering glass. Scharf and I pivoted to see a man in a hockey jersey leaping into the air and screaming with joy as he smashed the window of a curio shop and jumped through it, mowing through a stationary display.

She squeezed my arm and pulled me away. “We have to go.”

“You’re not going to play the hero?”

She shook her head. “There are no heroes tonight.”

We ran toward the end of the block, past restaurant after restaurant. I didn’t know how any of them stayed in business, considering all the competition.

When we reached the end, Scharf turned left, further away from the car, but I didn’t argue. As more glass shattered behind us, I realized she was right; one person could never stop this. I pushed the thought away that maybe Jack could have.

The crowds were thinner here. The metro and most of the parking structures were in the other direction, so we could pick up our pace. My pounding heart didn’t slow; from this distance, the crowd sounded like a roaring beast.

We passed a bank and a teashop, and someone screamed. Scharf spun, her hand on her weapon. I reached through my fatigue and fear for my magic.

“He’s dead!” A guy raced into the street from the darkened alley beside the teashop.

3

“Who’s dead?” Scharf asked as we skidded to a stop.

The guy turned his tear-stained, grief-ravaged face toward us and repeated, “He’s dead!”

I rubbed my hands together, feeling extra strength build in them, grateful again that I no longer had to make a bargain or ask anyone for permission. Magic arrived on command.

I really hoped I didn’t need it. Up close, I could see he was young, maybe even still a teenager. He wore what I thought of as generic restaurant attire. Jack wore it often: jeans, dirty sneakers, a beat-up long-sleeved shirt, and an apron. His hair was tucked into a bandana around his forehead.

“Sir, are you in danger?” Scharf called out. “What happened?”

“He’s dead!”

“Where?” Scharf drew her weapon. She did a sweep of the street and pointed with the muzzle of her Glock towards the alley he’d run out of.

“In the alley?” She got in his face. “Sir, in the alley? Was there anyone else? Did you see anyone?”

“N-no!”

She dashed towards the dark, skinny opening between the bank and an Italian restaurant, weapon by her side, sweeping the area like she had the spectators at the game.

“What about backup?” I shouted just before she disappeared.

“This is not a riot,” she yelled over her shoulder and ducked down the alley.

I scanned the street, trying to spot a murderer. A couple of hockey fans looked at us, but none of the other spectators surging down the street paused. One thing I didn’t have was super senses. I could barely see through the rain, let alone sense lurking danger.

We were in front of a series of three-story brownstones that had been turned into shops: a music shop, a teashop, a pasta joint, and a bank. One trash can at the corner was still overflowing with garbage, but overall the street was much cleaner than nearer the arena.

The music shop had an awning, and I guided the guy under it so we were out of the rain, which had slackened to a miserable drizzle. There was a dim light from the teashop, but the music shop was completely dark. I did another sweep of the street. I couldn’t shake the conviction we were being watched.

“You’re paranoid,” I murmured and chafed my hands, wishing I could manipulate fire instead of earth.

Finally, I couldn’t take the suspense and got up to stand at the mouth of the alley despite the rain, so I could keep Scharf in sight. The kid stood under the awning, trembling.

The gap between the buildings was barely wide enough for a car to pass through, and a couple of dumpsters obscured the view to the end. Sickly yellow light illuminated a short stretch of pavement from the open door at the side of the pasta joint. Something—someone—lay in the light on the ground. I swallowed hard against a jolt of nausea and jogged back to the kid.

Scharf clomped back around the corner moments later, her phone glued to her ear.

“Yeah, we’re at H and Sixth. It’s behind…” She pivoted to take in her surroundings. The music shop didn’t seem to have a name; the tea shop sign said Long Yinyue, and the restaurant had a huge glowing cursive light bolted into the brick that spelled Pasta Joe’s. “A restaurant. Yeah, crime scene, Metro PD, BAU, and my office. I’ll call there next and get another agent out here for the initial investigation.”

I frowned. Scharf worked violent crimes, and usually, local law enforcement called her in, not the other way around. She only got involved if the case involved federal charges. A murder in an alley outside Chinatown didn’t seem like a federal case to me, but right now she was trying to roll out her office and the local police and, judging from the acronyms, a couple of other agencies. What exactly was in the alley?

“Yeah, I’m sure it was a fork. What do you want me to say?”

I frowned and mouthed, A fork?

She turned to the kid. “What’s your name?”

It took him a second to focus on her.

I went over and patted him on the shoulder. “I know this is hard. Can you tell us your name?”

He shook like a tree in a hurricane and didn’t seem to hear.

“Focus!” I said, trying to imbue some of my magic into my voice. I borrowed some of Scharf’s calm to give to him. My magic came from two sources. The first was the earth, helpful in rescuing nachos. But I wasn’t just a goblin, I was a more powerful sort called a hobgoblin. I could also draw power from humanity. It was useful in the face of a panicky human. It was this power to hob that I threw at the kid.

Blinking like I’d given him a lobotomy, he said, “I don’t know.”

Scharf looked at me with narrowed eyes. “What did you do?”

“Nothing.”

“And by nothing you mean…”

“I tried to calm him down with a little magic…” I winced as her eyebrows drew together with every word I said. “He’s calm now, right?”

“Orange.”

“Sorry.”

“Don’t help me,” she said firmly.

Carefully, I told my magic to stop, and he started shaking again. I wanted to take all the pain and fear away but knew that wasn’t possible.

“What’s your name?” I asked again.

“David. David Bao.”

Scharf put a hand on David’s thin shoulder. “You work at one of these shops? I saw the spilled garbage. You were taking out the garbage?”

He nodded, pointing to the teashop. “My aunt and uncle run it. Rodger works next door. I’ve known him for years.” He sounded, fortunately, a little less magicked, though significantly more upset.

“Are your aunt and uncle here? Anyone else in the building?”

He shook his head. “They’re out with friends in Maryland. I was just closing up. Rodger did the same. He works next door. We were going to get a beer.”

As David stared towards the alley, I asked Scharf, “What’s going on?”

She hissed and shook her head. I told David not to notice me.

“He can’t hear me,” I said. “What’s in that alley?”

“Nothing for you to help with. A run-of-the-mill homicide.”

David moaned, folded in on himself, collapsing onto the step of the music shop. I cringed; I’d forgotten I wasn’t touching Scharf, so she was perfectly visible and audible.

“I’m so sorry,” she told David.

He launched to his feet and made for the alley, too quick even for Scharf. As she sprinted after him, I grabbed him with magic and held him until Scharf could grab the back of his shirt.

“You don’t want to see that again, David.”

Her comment about the fork came back to me. You’ve got to be kidding me.

Sirens wailed, and a single squad car parted the spectators and screeched to a halt at the curb.

Scharf pointed to David as she strode towards the curb and whispered. “Keep him out here.”

I patted his shoulder, still holding him with magic. “I’m so sorry!”

“I can’t believe it,” he murmured, staring at the squad car. “I just can’t believe it.”

“One car?” Scharf asked the officer heaving himself out of the driver’s seat.

The burly officer, dressed in the dark blue of Metro PD, shook his head. “Lady, there’s a riot out there. You called about one stupid murder.”

David moaned again, and the cop rolled his eyes.

“First of all, the name’s ‘Agent,’ not ‘Lady.’ Special Agent Laura Scharf, FBI, Violent Crimes.”

“Agent, ma’am, you still ain’t getting any more officers for a few hours. At least.”

“Even when the Restaurant Killer’s back?”

David gasped like someone punched him in the stomach.

“Come with me,” she said, leading the officer towards the alley.

“Who’s the Restaurant Killer?” I called after her.

The cop answered, “John Hastings, the serial killer who murdered six people in Baltimore a few years ago.”

“What he said,” Scharf said, then pointed to the officer. “You’re going to want to get your bosses on this.”

He shook his head. “We caught the Restaurant Killer. Hastings is at the Chesapeake supermax. We caught him.”

“I know his signature, and some…” She glanced at David. “Some details were held from the press.”

The officer shook his head violently. “We caught him!”

“We caught somebody,” Scharf said.

4

I leaned against a dumpster thirty feet from an alphabet soup of agencies standing over the body, all of whom were debating what to do as the rain flooded the alley with mud, grease, and spilled trash.

Rodger was barely older than David or me, with pale skin and a shock of blond hair. His body was in child’s pose, with his knees tucked under his torso and his arms outstretched as if supplicating for his life, just across the threshold of the back door of the pasta shop.

I’d gotten a glance before I’d retreated out of the alley. It was only my second dead body, and the first had been in a morgue, cleaned up, and was still far more than I could cope with.

The first cop to pull up had taken David to a precinct, and I’d then gone completely invisible as more and more law enforcement arrived: techs in coveralls, more uniforms, and the FBI in their dark blue jackets with yellow letters. I hid under the awning of the music shop and shivered as the rain worsened.

Yesterday was sunny and warm for winter, but that was over. I debated leaving, but I wanted to help. That was the bargain I’d made with Scharf only a month ago: I’d help her when her case brushed the magical world in exchange for a place to live.

Scharf strode out of the alley; she’d gotten an umbrella from somewhere and twirled it as she dug out her phone, put it to her ear, and said, “You can probably go home.”

When I said nothing, she stared at me.

“Oh! You mean me. I should go home.”

“Is anyone else here?” she asked.

“I thought you were on the phone… Never mind.” I jammed my hands in my armpits to keep warm.

“The Bureau’s taking point on the case for now since this is most likely connected to the other Restaurant Killer cases we’ve worked on, so I’m going to be here a while.”

“I could help.”

Scharf smirked, one corner of her mouth ticking up. “No offense, Orange, but we’re pretty sure this is a regular human murderer. We’re waiting for the medical examiner.”

“How do you know it was him? Your Restaurant Killer?”

“There’s a fork in the victim’s left eyeball. It was his calling card.”

“More than one person can stick somebody in the eye with a fork.” I pushed my hair out of my face. It drooped around my head in the driving rain.

“Yeah, but can they strangle him, pose him exactly like the other victims, then stick a fork in his eye?”

“Ew.”

Scharf shrugged. “There’s more. David said Rodger wore a medical alert bracelet. It’s missing. The killer always took jewelry or a ring off the victim’s hands. I studied this killer when I first got to DC. He did this for years.”

“But you said you caught him.”

“Yeah, John Hastings. He got angry with his boss when he was working at a fast-food joint. His first kill was that boss, and then he kept going.”

“But if this is the same killer, none of that’s right.”

She sighed and brushed a hand over her hair, pushing the water out of it. “You’re right, but there hasn’t been a murder like this in the four years since we locked him up.”

“So if it wasn’t him, it could be a supernatural creature,” I suggested, surprised by my FOMO. I would have said investigating crimes was the last thing I wanted to do with my life, but here I was with an out, and I was arguing to stay.

“Just go home, Orange.”

“You’re my ride.”

“You’re a goblin.”

As I thought about her house in Virginia, an orange line showed up in my vision. No one else could see it but me, and it showed me the way home like magical Google Maps; it involved a Metro and two buses. “I’m good. I’ll wait.”

“Scharf, we meet again.”

We pivoted to see a guy in a trench coat get out of a beat-up sedan. As he turned our way, I groaned.

“Lee!” Scharf called out.

Detective Lee was Metro PD and had arrested me a couple of times already—once for loitering and once for domestic terrorism. He was pushing forty with a slight Southern accent and was slowly losing the fight between abs and pudge. He filled out a familiar rumpled blue blazer under a more rumpled trench coat. His black hair was in a sharp buzz cut, though, and his badge was on his belt.

“If this is that Restaurant Killer y’all thought you caught, what am I doing here?”

“Liaising,” Scharf replied in a flat drawl.

“Translated from Fed speak,” Lee said, “that means dumping all the paperwork on me?”

Scharf snorted. “I don’t make the rules. Guy’s dead, so we call in the detectives.”

Scharf looked at the milling crime scene techs. “Um, Happy New Year, by the way. If that’s what you say. If you celebrate. Your family is from Korea, right? Never mind.” She trailed off.

“‘Happy new year’ works. We did celebrate Gujeong today. And no, I didn’t go to the parade. Nobody goes to the parade.” He threw a hand toward Chinatown. “Obviously. And then the Feds called me to a crime scene to look at a guy with a fork in his eye.”

I felt compelled to repeat myself. “Ew.”

Scharf, the only one who could hear me, twitched.

“So, what’s the story?” Lee asked.

Scharf rocked back on her heels. “The victim, Rodger Clark, is an employee at the scene. Nineteen years old. Resident of Virginia. Found by David Bao, an employee of the Long Yinyue Teashop next door. Pasta owner has been contacted.”

“The Joe of Pasta Joe’s?” Lee asked.

“Just so. He says he’s in a bar in Virginia with a bunch of friends watching the hockey game on TV.”

“Convenient.”

She shook her head. “One of the friends is a judge.”

“So probably not the owner. Have we found the new hire?”

“What new hire?” I asked before I remembered he couldn’t hear me.

Scharf took a deep breath. “The killer always started out working for the business, gained the boss’s trust, and then murdered the boss. Reenacting his revenge for the injustice he suffered at the hands of his original boss.”

Lee blinked. “Yeah, I know. I mean, it wasn’t my case since it was too fancy for us.”

“And he crossed state lines?” Scharf offered mildly.

“But I kept up with it. We haven’t had a serial in a long time.”

“No new hire that we know about yet. The rest of the pattern fits.”

Lee dug his hands into his trench coat pockets. “Except all his victims were older than fifty to match the first boss. And he’s never come this far south. And all served American food, not Italian.” Lee ticked off each point on his fingers.

Scharf threw up her hands. “It’s been an hour. Give me a break.”

Lee grinned, crinkling the corner of his eyes. “So, how do you want to work it? I’ll get people canvassing. See what they saw.”

“The medical examiner’s assistant is on the way,” Scharf said.

“What about the kid who found him? No chance he’s pulling a copycat?”

“Look, without the fork, I would say he’s the prime suspect—no alibi, definitely onsite…”

I gasped. Could that heartbroken, traumatized kid seriously strangle his friend and then run for help? I couldn’t imagine it.

“But we kept those details back from the press. He doesn’t have any connection to law enforcement that I can find to learn those details. But we’re looking.”

Lee scoffed. “True crime podcasts, the trial itself, it was all over the internet!”

Scharf shook her head. “No, it’s too perfect—the body, I mean. You have to see it. There are details we didn’t even share with you guys, let alone the media.”

“Now that’s not very nice, holding out on us.” Lee nudged her biceps with his elbow and walked deeper into the crime scene as they chatted. I moved to follow them, but Scharf held up a finger behind her back.

“You okay?” Lee asked, cocking his head at the finger.

She dropped her hand. “Just testing the wind.”

“Why?”

Scharf raised both eyebrows and glanced at me. “Don’t you know how wind can affect a crime scene?”

“In an alley?”

She cleared her throat to say something else when a loud voice from behind us announced, “This way, sir.”

Everyone turned to see three newcomers enter the alley. Two of them were not dressed for a crime scene or the weather. The third was another familiar face.

I sighed. James Carlisle was an FBI agent who worked with Scharf in violent crimes. I referred to him as the underwear model because he was beautiful, blond, and chiseled, but he’d also undercut Scharf by blaming her for things that had gone wrong in their last case and didn’t look so beautiful anymore.

“Who are they?” Lee asked Scharf.

“That’s Carlisle, out of my office. I don’t know the others.”

The FBI didn’t have regular partners. They had squads where the agent in charge of a case would pull in whoever else was available, but she ended up with Carlisle a lot. He’d only been assigned to her field office a few months before, straight from the FBI Academy, and she was sort of training him.

He was currently simpering at the other two with him, which was new. I also called him the underwear model because humility was not one of his… values.

One was an older man, as buff as the rest of the agents, which told me he also probably worked for the FBI. His full head of silver hair was raked away from his face and seemed impervious to the sleeting rain.

His companion was a woman at least half his age with a helmet of dark-red hair that came from a bottle. Carlisle kept his hand hovering at her lower back as he escorted her down the alley.

The old dude scanned the entire scene with keen eyes. As his gaze paused on me, my heart flipped over. But his gray eyes swept on. Once they did, I exhaled slowly.

“Yes,” he said. “This is the killer’s regular hunting grounds.”

Nobody moved. The investigators, including Scharf and Lee, just stared at the man.

Lee muttered, “I didn’t know Baltimore stretched so far south.”

“And you are…?” Scharf asked.

“Agent Richard Fuller with BAU, and this is Melissa, my niece. She’s writing, well, she’s writing about my life and my 29 years in the FBI. I trust her presence at the crime scene won’t be an impediment?”

The blood left Scharf’s face, her only reaction for a second before she jumped forward.

“Agent Fuller! You still work cases?” she said with very un-Scharf enthusiasm.

“I haven’t in a couple of years. They wouldn’t even wave the retirement age for me, but if the Restaurant Killer’s still at large… Well, I had to leave Quantico and the classroom they’ve stuck me in and make sure you young sprigs don’t drop the ball.”

“It’s so amazing that the killer has struck again!” Melissa said, struggling to keep her little notebook dry. “I’ll need to interview everyone onsite! For the book.”

“You are?” Fuller asked.

“Scharf,” Carlisle said. “The one I told you about who works with me.”

“Violet crimes, right?” Fuller asked. I snickered. The N had fallen off the door of Scharf’s office and now even the Bureau was calling it violet crimes.

“It’s actually… Yes sir,” Scharf said quietly. “It’s an honor to meet you.”

“Show me this body!” Fuller said but did not wait for Scharf to move and strode by her, his niece trotting behind him.

As we followed, I asked, “BAU?”

Scharf whispered, “Behavioral Analysis Unit. Think Jodie Foster in Silence of the Lambs.”

I gulped, fighting my tightening shoulders and throat. This was spiraling way beyond a regular murder. Scharf jogged to get ahead of Fuller. “Let me take you through the crime scene, Agent.”

“Please, call me Rick.”

“Rick,” Lee said, his voice flat as he followed them as well. It was clear he wasn’t nearly as impressed with this dude as Scharf seemed to be. “How’d you get here so fast?”

“We were in the neighborhood this evening! Melissa and I were at the arena to attend the game that precipitated the riot.”

Melissa picked her way daintily down the trash-strewn alley in her high heels. “Sometimes you just get lucky.”

I frowned. “How incredibly lucky that somebody died. Just the best luck.”

My phone rang, and Scharf looked at me with fear in her eyes, but no one else did. I snapped, “They can’t hear it!”

Like my clothes and anything I carried, any electronics stayed unnoticed too.

I checked the screen and put the phone to my ear. “Jack! Where are you? Did you go out that window?”

“Looking for you!” Jack said. “Did you get out of the riot?”

“Scharf and I are okay. We got out.”

“Where are you? I’ll come get you.”

I spun, taking in the rain-soaked alley and the officer unspooling crime-scene tape across the entrance. “Home. I went home already,” I said, wanting to feel guilty about lying to him, but I just didn’t. “Sorry. Scharf said you could take care of yourself.”

“Scharf did, did she?” Fortunately, he sounded amused rather than angered.

“Jack, you’re a demon. You expect me to buy that you were in any kind of danger?”

Scharf had been saying something to Fuller, but stopped talking and looked at me in horror. I grimaced and sloshed away from her. The demon was out of the closet.

“This isn’t about me,” Jack said. “I was worried about you!”

“I’m a goblin! True, I don’t pack the same punch you do, but you were worried about me? Seriously?” It outraged the feminist half of me that he thought I couldn’t take care of myself; the other half was flattered that he wanted to protect me. I sighed. “Thanks, I guess.”

“When can I see you again?”

I grinned. “You’re terrible at playing hard to get.”

“Why? Easy is much more fun.”

“Give me the download,” Fuller said.

“I’ll call you,” I told Jack, then hung up.

Scharf gestured around the alley. “As you can see, we cordoned off the crime scene, and we’re going to preserve everything in the alley. The Restaurant Killer always left the rope he used to strangle his victims and his clothes in the dumpsters out back. We haven’t found those yet.”

“He always did like to play with us,” Fuller said, trying to suppress his glee.

Lee and I rolled our eyes. This dude was positively thrilled to be standing in the alley with a strangled teenager.

“We’re going to get somebody in tomorrow to drain the dumpster,” Scharf added “Looks like there are at least two fresh bags in there, but with the rain coming down now—”

“Not tomorrow!” Fuller said. “If we wait until then, vital pieces of evidence could be lost.”

Carlisle, still hovering around the redhead, said, “Excellent point, sir.”

“We know how to work a scene,” Lee muttered.

Scharf cleared her throat as she turned to Fuller. “We weigh that against what we might miss in the dark and the rain. We can take the whole dumpster to our lab and comb through it all.”

“Absolutely not. We have to go through this tonight, Agent. Get in there. Now!”

“Scharf? Get in there. Now.” Carlisle grinned, his hand on Melissa’s waist. She scribbled madly in a notebook that was getting soggier by the second.

Scharf looked at the dumpster, then at Fuller. Her eyebrows pulled together. “The truck’s going to be here any minute. We’ll make sure all of this gets to the lab. It’s taken care of, Rick.”

Fuller shook his head. “Nothing is taken care of that you don’t take care of yourself.”

Lee chewed on his upper lip and crossed his arms. “You want her to perform the autopsy too?”

“Agent Fuller is right,” Scharf said. “Don’t pass the buck.” She looked at the dumpster and swallowed. Was she really going to climb in there?

As she rolled up her sleeves, I stepped forward. The water needed to be drained out of the dumpster. I closed my eyes and tugged at the side of it. It was metal and no match for my magic.

Something weird happened, though, as I reached out in my mind’s eye to break it open and let the water out; I felt the water. But that was impossible—the element I could control was earth. I shook off the momentary awareness and pulled on the side of the dumpster. A wave of trash and water spilled all over the alley. Then it flooded into the back of the restaurant where the body lay.

Oops.

A dozen investigators squawked like chickens as they hopped around, trying to preserve the scene. Melissa, wearing heels, squealed and leaped. I was impressed; she made it a good four feet even in her impractical outfit.

The profiler didn’t panic. He stepped onto a milk crate and tsked at everyone and everything. “This is why you don’t wait. Now we’ve lost vital evidence.”

I froze. Lost vital evidence?

Scharf had been closest to the dumpster and was completely soaked in water and trash. She wiped at the grey muck on her pants; I could smell the rot from ten feet away. “I don’t know what happened,” she said. “I was certain the dumpster was sound. I checked it myself.”

I backed up.

“I’ll be right back,” she said and strode after me.

I sped up, heading for the crime scene tape and an officer in a transparent raincoat standing at the mouth of the alley. But she reached me before I could get out.

“What did you do?” she asked.

“Nothing!”

She put her hands on her soaked hips. “Did you rip off the side of the dumpster?”

I really wanted to lie to her. So I did. “No.”

“Orange Sinclair!”

“Okay, fine.”

She let out a deep sigh. “You destroyed the crime scene. God knows what we lost. The killer always leaves his gloves in a trash bag behind the restaurant. He wants to make the investigators dig through the trash.”

I spread my hands. “I was just trying to help.”

“If I want your help, I will ask,” she snapped, with more emotion than she’d shown before. “The next time we get fairy dust or a little creature running through a crime scene, you’ll be the first person I call. But right now, go home, bake some scones, and stay out of my job. You almost got me fired once already.”

“That was not me,” I pointed out to her. A djinni almost got her fired when she was put on administrative leave after getting too close to him.

Her face changed colors, and two red stripes appeared on her cheeks from her clenched teeth.

I retreated, and an orange line appeared behind me. My magic wanted to skedaddle. “Okay, I’m going,”

“Please don’t do magic at crime scenes.”

“Don’t worry!”

As I stomped off, Lee said, “Everything okay, Scharf?”

“Yeah fine, why?”

“You were talking to yourself pretty loudly over here.”

“Worse, I was talking to my invisible friend who won’t do what I say and go home.” She tossed that last remark over her shoulder at me.

I stuck out my tongue.

Lee frowned. “Has anyone ever disappeared from your custody? Literally disappeared?”

I hunched. I’d done that to him accidentally the first time I’d discovered my powers of invisibility. Ever since I’d tried to appear or disappear out of sight of those not in the know.

Scharf glanced at me. “Never.”

Lee shook it off and brushed the rain off his shoulders. “Let’s try to get all the trash picked up, okay? Something tells me this hotshot and his biographer won’t stoop to that.”

“Ah, the glamour, the danger, the rewards of law enforcement.”

I thought to offer to pick it all up myself but didn’t think Scharf would appreciate that. I walked away, following my orange line to the Metro. I’d screwed up the crime scene and gotten Scharf in trouble, so my work there was done. Normally, we had a great working relationship. I’d joke; she’d solve crimes and pretend not to be amused.

She hadn’t been pretending tonight.

5

As I sat on a yellow line Metro train winding its way south towards Virginia, I told myself the moisture streaming down my face was only rain. And my hands were shaking because of the cold.

I enjoyed pushing Scharf’s buttons. She was so starchy and correct that almost anything did it, but this time she had gotten so angry, so dismissive. I was used to that from teachers and boyfriends, but not from her. She’d been one of the first people to take me seriously. I’d underestimated how much I liked that.

And she was totally wrong. I wasn’t sleeping on her couch for free; I was sleeping on her couch in exchange for helping her. Making myself agreeable and having a place to sleep were intimately connected, and so her anger felt much bigger than the fight. She told you to go home.

I crossed my arms to control their shaking as I finally warmed up. I knew little about the Lunar New Year versus the one in December, the one I hated so much because of its annual reminder of my lack of progress in life, but this was a terrible start to both of them. I thought back over the hockey game and how the crowd turned. What had provoked them?

I would go home. Then we would patch this up in the morning, and she could investigate this thing herself.

This late, the only people in the car with me were a businessman who’d worked way too late and a homeless dude sleeping across one seat. As we hurtled south, thoughts of the Restaurant Killer kept intruding. For my folklore major, I’d learned about a lot of monsters, and I’d recently hung out with a few as well. But I still couldn’t imagine who could do that to another human being.

“Leave it alone,” I told the poster across from my seat advertising some kind of polar star defense system. The yellow line passed the Pentagon on its way south, and sometimes the advertising got a little patriotic. Or paranoid.

Five minutes later, as soon as we’d pulled above ground to cross the Potomac into Virginia, I pulled out my phone and searched for the Restaurant Killer. The first result was a book, Monster in the Kitchen, by Melissa Fuller, the redhead with the notebook who’d wormed her way onto the crime scene. The cover of the book had a picture of an alley with a chalk outline. I shuddered and downloaded it. She’d written it just after the trial.

I quailed just reading the table of contents. There were six chapters dedicated to the autopsies alone, and another four to the trial. I flipped to chapter one. She summarized Richard Fuller’s years in the FBI, first in violent crimes like Scharf, then in the Behavioral Analysis Unit out of Quantico. He worked on high-profile murders around the country, consulting with police before the Restaurant Killer case had skyrocketed him to fame when people had started getting strangled in the backs of restaurants, arranged artfully where they had fallen with a fork in their eye.

I gulped. Melissa used the word “artfully.”

A rumble of thunder drew my eyes to the horizon and the storm clouds gathering in the ever-present orange glow of the city. It had been raining steadily for hours, but this was something else. The clouds looked alive, like someone had pressed “fast-forward” on the storm, and it was hurtling right toward us. I brushed against the pole at the back of my seat as I leaned towards the window and jumped as static electricity fizzed beneath my fingertips.

I didn’t want to be out when that storm hit. I tried to tell myself I was being fanciful, but one thing I’d learned quickly once my goblin powers had manifested was that I wasn’t fanciful. It was dangerous to dismiss my instincts.

The homeless dude got out at the next stop, ranting about the horror of the rain. Nobody likes thunderstorms; maybe I was picking up on the collective bracing of humanity against the weather.

“Hello?” a voice asked.

I yanked my eyes away from the storm, scanning the car.

The dude in a suit and trench coat sat in the seat facing away from me, looking cold and exhausted. He had big earphones over his ears.

“Can you see me?”

I glanced down. A goblin stepped around the seats and into view. He was shorter than my five feet by an additional foot with deep blue skin and blue hair cut in a mullet.

“Yes, I can see you?”

“Are you the Peallaidh?” He pronounced my father’s name as “Peal.” I didn’t correct him. Scots Gaelic is hard for anyone to pronounce. My father always rounded it out, so it sounded more like “powlich.”

The goblin wore a kid’s shirt with a collection of superheroes on the front over a sparkly pair of jeans meant for a little girl. He smelled like rotting beer. I didn’t take my eyes off him. You never knew what kind of power might concentrate in small packages. I was proof enough of that.

“Are you the Peallaidh?” he asked again. His voice was high and thin.

I sighed and told him what he wanted to hear. “Yes. I’m your leader person. Whatever we’re calling it.”

I was glad we could conceal what we were because if anybody saw him walking around on any other night except Halloween, they’d run screaming to the nearest government science lab. And since we were in downtown DC, there were plenty of those.

I told the guy in headphones not to notice me, just in case, and said to the goblin, “What can I do for you?”

“I challenge thee.” He made a fist and pounded his chest over his heart.

“You’re too late. I already claimed the box.” When my father died a few months ago, he’d left a box for me that held his powers as the leader of goblins. I still hadn’t been able to open the thing, but since I’d claimed it, the world of magical creatures treated me like I was his successor. I still wasn’t sure what that meant, but I had a feeling I was about to find out.

“You want me to bow?” he asked, his nose curling until I could see his nostrils.

“Tell me whether you’re supposed to bow, and I’ll know whether to be offended if you haven’t.”

“I refuse. I refuse to bow to the—upstart.” He pounded his chest again and repeated, “I challenge thee.”

“You said that already. Why?”

He scratched between his eyes. “Why?”

“Yeah, why are you challenging me? My father was on top and passed whatever was in the box to me. Not you.” Magical creature governance structures were stuck in the Middle Ages. I shared my father’s blood, so I was next in line.

“My job is to challenge you!” he said.

“Job?” I narrowed my eyes. “Someone’s paying you?”

He shook his head. “Enough talking; we have to fight.” He clenched his fists at his sides, and I seriously hoped he didn’t mean to punch me.

I reached for my magic, even as I gave him a big grin, still trying to find a way out of this. “My name is Orange. Maybe we could chat first?”

“I am Everglades Park.” He went to pound his chest again but winced and rubbed his chest.

“Your name is Everglades?”

“It is the house I serve.”

“Let me guess. You’re from Florida?”

“Yes?” He looked gobsmacked that I had guessed a goblin named Everglades came from Florida.

He didn’t serve the family; he served the house. Most goblins ruled over and kept homes and were beholden to bargains with their owners for magic, while I had a built-in source from my human half. In all the stories I’d read about goblins, we assisted humans or made their lives miserable, depending on what they did for us. It was a big deal that this little guy had uprooted and come after me.

I bit my lip to keep from giggling. I’d never met a real-life Florida Man. Though I guess he would be a Florida Goblin?

“Now we fight.” He stuck one fist in the air, avoiding his chest, and then half-heartedly poked it in my face.

I tilted my head to dodge it. In hindsight, I shouldn’t have been surprised. Of course, the goblins get a new leader and challenge her. Why couldn’t we all have a nice holiday feast and passive-aggressively mention politics or religion while we stuffed ourselves?

He tried to punch me again, coming a whole six inches closer to my nose.

“If I fight back, we’re going to derail this train. Can’t we fight somewhere else?”

Everglades snarled and finally summoned the courage to leap at me. I batted him out of the way, my heart in my throat.

He crashed into the wheelchair seat, which swings upward to accommodate someone’s chair. Instead, it crashed downward, dangling from its hinges. I realized I was in trouble, not because he was any sort of challenge, but because he was so much weaker than me. I didn’t want to hurt him.

I didn’t want to do this.

He roared from the ground and snapped his fingers, and all the seat cushions leaped into the air and slammed together. Fortunately, they missed the human on the train, who clearly had his eyes closed and seemed oblivious to the supernatural fight happening around him. Those were some quality headphones.

“Why are you doing this?” I asked as I knocked them to the floor.

“I challenge thee!” His dark hair was waving around his head, and he’d turned a darker shade of blue in his anger.

“Save me from goblins,” I muttered and tried to put the seat cushions back in the right place, but it was hopeless.

He roared and leaped again, his claws extended. They weren’t really claws, just pointy nails. Mine grew in the same way, and I knew from experience how much they hurt when they gouged into flesh.

He landed on my shoulder and broke the skin, tearing through my new red dress. Hardening my skin to heal it, I growled, “When was the last time you washed your hands?”

“I challenge thee!”

“Would you stop saying that?” I batted him away again but couldn’t control his fall.

He slammed into another seat, this time laying waste to the entire metal frame that supported it. I cringed, terrified I’d killed him.

The goblin got to his feet and shook his head, his black eyes wheeling in his wrinkled face. He was upright, but he wasn’t okay.

“Why aren’t you all helping me?” I asked. Fresh anger warred with an old ache of isolation. I hadn’t yet met a goblin who was happy to see me. “Why do we always have to fight?”

“I challenge—”

I sighed. “Challenge me. I know. What will make this stop?”

He leaped.

I couldn’t dodge, and he latched on to the front of me in an obscene version of a hug. I had an unpleasant minute getting him off. As I pried his hands from around my middle, he ripped the front pocket of my jeans, making this the fourth outfit I’d ruined in the line of duty. “Not the jeans! They fit!”

I picked the goblin up with my mind and dangled him above the slushy floor. “Don’t make me throw you out the window!” I said, hoping a more dire threat would get through to him. “Stop challenging me.”

With round eyes and limbs flailing, he looked at the floor and then at me. “How? You didn’t get time to make a bargain for power.”

“I’m half-human, you—you goblin! I don’t need to bargain for my magic. I can bargain with myself.” A grin crept across my face. I hadn’t yet met another half-human goblin, and there was a serious advantage about being both, though it had taken me a while to figure that out.

As the truth of that settled on him, Everglades hung limply in my bonds. “You’re human?”

I didn’t know if it was longing, jealousy, or fury that transfigured his face. I looked around the hurricane-stricken car and sighed. “Are you going to stop challenging the—me?”

He nodded, and his mouth drooped.

“I’m not letting you down until you say it.” Honestly, I didn’t know what he was supposed to say but judging from his, “I challenge thee” repetition, there had to be something to end this officially.

Thunder boomed outside the windows, and rain pelted the glass with new fury. “And now the storm is here. Fantastic.”

He looked up at me in confusion. “Aren’t you the Peallaidh?”

“How’s that supposed to keep me from being soaked?”

“You are the Peallaidh. Stop the rain,” he said matter-of-factly.

“Stop it?” I scoffed. “I only have earth powers. In case you haven’t noticed.”

“But—”

I shook him and felt myself tiring. He needed to capitulate quickly. “Are you done with your little challenge thing?”

The goblin nodded. “I am defeated.”

Trying to judge whether he was sincere, I left him up there for a moment. When nothing happened, I said, “Okay, then.”

I let go of his bonds, and he tumbled to the floor and scampered away.

“Hey, stop. Where are you going?”

He turned around. “I am defeated.”

“So?”

“I must now go drown myself.”

I put my hands up. “Whoa. No drowning, dude. Nobody’s gonna drown around here.”

“I am—”

“Defeated. So you said. You don’t have to jump into the Potomac! Stop.” He stopped. “You’re one of the few goblins I’ve met in my life. Do you want to get ice cream or something?” I felt like a kid asking someone on a date.

He tugged on the end of one ear. Like most goblins, including me, his ears were long, pointy, and jagged. “You wanna eat with me?” he said.

“Yeah, I would.”

“I am not worthy!” he cried and bounded off.

I called out, “I’ll make a deal!”

He screeched to a stop.

“I’ll make a deal,” I repeated, and walked toward him, dodging seat cushions. No goblin could resist a deal. “Don’t even think about drowning yourself, and we’ll share food. You’ll be worthy if it’s a bargain, right?” Looking terrified, he shook his head, so I said, “Okay, never mind. How about the opposite? You don’t drown yourself, and I promise never to ask you to share food again.”

He didn’t say anything but didn’t walk away either.

“Say ‘deal,’” I told him.

He nodded.

I shook my head. “Say ‘deal.’”

“Deal,” he said, staring at the floor and shuffling his feet. He looked and sounded like a recalcitrant child. The chime in my head signaled we had a bargain.

“Thank you for not dying because of me,” I said with fervent sincerity.

He looked up at me, his expression serious. “You’d best avoid the others then.”

My hand slipped to my side in shock. “The others?”

“My brothers.”

Oh, no. “You have brothers?”

“Every other goblin in your territory.”

“What’s my territory?”

“They are coming for you. They must challenge the Peallaidh.”

My jaw tumbled open. “But I have the box. I claimed it.”

He nodded eagerly. “We know. We felt it. Like when we felt the old Peallaidh die. May he live forever. That’s why we all have to come.”

I decided not to query why he wished eternal life on a dead man. “So I’m going to be attacked on a regular basis?”

He gave half a shrug and tipped over. In fatigue or shock, I wasn’t sure.

As he righted himself, I asked, “When’s it going to stop?”

“When we know you are worthy.”

“And how will you know that?”

“When you defeat all of us.”

My heart stopped for a full two seconds. “How big is my territory? Can I get a map? Or even better, a census?” I bit my lip to keep myself from begging. “I did not sign up for this.”

He blinked twice. “Yes, you did. You claimed your legacy. You’re the Peallaidh.”

I sat down on the floor. “There has to be a shortcut.”

He bobbed. “You could go straight to the top. Find the most powerful, fight him, and then anybody weaker will know you defeated him, and then…” He seemed to get lost in his argument, but I got the gist.

“Who’s the most powerful goblin in my undefined territory? Besides me?”

He bit his tongue. It was a mottled purple after the fight, and I looked away.

“It might be Billy Blind,” he said. “No, I think the Lubber Fiend defeated him.”

“And how would I find this Lubber Fiend?” I wanted to ask how to defeat him too, but somehow that seemed like cheating.

Cheating’s fine! I told myself. Why not cheat?

“Well, we could bargain, and I could find him for you.”

I frowned, trying to see the catch. There was always a catch. “Okay? What’s in it for you?”

“You let me have honor in my defeat, and I will let the Lubber Fiend know you are waiting.”

“No drowning yourself.”

“Deal.”

I cleared my throat and said, “Deal.”

When he ran straight for the window of the car, I found the catch. He’d promised not to drown himself, but there were dozens of ways to die. We were hurtling south on elevated tracks. He’d be completely squished. He took a flying leap, punched through the glass of the door between cars, and did an elegant swan dive into the rain.

6

“No!” I screamed and leaped fifteen feet to the end of the car, but still wasn’t fast enough. I tried to reach the goblin with my magic before he became a pancake, but for the second time that night, I was too late.

Something thumped on the tracks, and I just stopped. I didn’t look down as the train slowed. We had been less than a hundred feet from a platform.

But I had to look when I heard a screeching cackle from below.

Impossibly, Everglades stood on the sidewalk below the tracks, waving.

“I have leaped from a moving train,” he cried out. I could hear him perfectly through the window he busted and the storm. “My honor is restored!”

I crossed my arms. “If you’d jumped into the river,” I yelled down to him, “would you have actually died?”

“Why would I kill myself? How would that restore my honor?”

“So if I lose to the Lubber Fiend, I can jump out a window?”

His expression turned grim. “Oh, no, when the apexes fight, it is death. The Lubber Fiend will kill. That one can’t live with a challenger.”

Oh, that’s just ducky.

The goblin ran away into the rain.

“Whoa!” someone shouted from behind me. “What the hell!”

I spun to see the passenger pull off his headphones amid the wreckage. I told him to forget me and slumped down in a seat as he quickly waded through the destroyed seats to the doors as we slowed down to reach the station. Our entire fight had only lasted a few minutes.

Maybe I should’ve started by challenging another midrange goblin. I’d hold them in the air until they agreed to jump off a bridge or out a window, and then I could work up to a fight to the death.

My phone buzzed, and I checked the screen and swiped to answer it.

“Astrid?” I said, “It’s really late or early—I don’t even know which one.”

“Juya is sick.”

Every hair on my body stood on end. Juya helped me when I first got to DC at the end of last year and any time I was in danger to this day. She was the only magical creature I’d encountered who wanted nothing from me. To hear she was sick was like hearing the statue of Abe at the Lincoln Memorial did a jig.

Of course, Astrid didn’t know that. She was a normal human college student who came to Juya’s for breakfast like I did most mornings.

“What happened?” I asked.

“I don’t know because she won’t go to a doctor. Nobody who lives with her is helping. Can you come? She listens to you.”

“They’re right—no doctors.” She was a magical creature, I knew that, but I didn’t know what kind or what might show up on a blood test.

“Never mind, don’t come! We need to get her to a doctor.”

“I’m coming.”

As the double doors opened, I dashed out, following the orange line that had materialized as soon as I said I’d come. A train returning to the city was pulling up on the opposite platform, and I sprinted around the dazed passenger who was trying to flag down the conductor at the front of the train, shouting about the destruction.

I ducked into the opposite train just before the doors closed, and slumped in the empty car, wet to the bone again just from the short dash and shivering hard.

At the next stop, a trio of drunk military dudes, judging by their buzzcuts, trooped on, crowing over catching the last train.

“How much?” one of them asked me.

I didn’t realize he was talking to me for a second, because I thought I was invisible.

He stepped closer. “Come on, doll, how much for a night?”

I rolled my eyes and told him to forget me, trying to decide whether to be offended or flattered by his offer.

“What the—did you see that, dude?” He waved a hand in my direction.

“What dude?”

“That chick disappeared!”

“You’re drunk!”

The car started moving, and they staggered into seats, still debating what he’d seen. I held my breath until they started razzing each other about striking out at the bar.

I was still getting used to the attention my glamour received. The real me with my orange skin and frizzy hair and flat chest did not get questionable offers from guys, no matter how drunk. My blond hair was apparently a real step up.

I jumped off the train at Columbia Heights, thankful I’d avoided any other challenges or offers. I jogged through the rain between gentrified big-box department stores into Juya’s neighborhood of coffee shops and Latin grocery stores a few blocks away.

Juya lived in a two-story 1920s townhouse ubiquitous in DC with a bunch of young relatives. Possibly they were just self-appointed helpers; I’d never gotten a clear picture of the web of relationships.

I froze when I hit her street. Her once-vibrant courtyard now looked derelict. The brick and wood townhouse was falling down between its identical twins on either side. I couldn’t breathe for several seconds until I remembered this is what Scharf and anyone else Juya didn’t want to talk to saw when they came here.

The fact that she was trying to ward off all comers—including myself—tripled my worry. The gate hung off its hinges, and the courtyard was full of debris. I knew it was an illusion, and the courtyard was full of chairs for her bakery customers, but when I kicked the gate with my toes, a soup can skittered across the concrete. Unlike my illusions, which lasted so long as you didn’t touch me, Juya’s went straight down to physical reality. Which begged the question, were they even illusions? And how could someone who could do this get sick?

I jogged up the broken steps to her townhouse and pounded on the door.

Astrid opened it, looking worried and sleep-deprived. She wore a bulky gray coat and gray sweatpants with Howard printed down one leg. She’d wrapped her normally impeccably styled hair in gray silk. It bemused me she’d coordinated her midnight outfit, though her face was bare of makeup and her dark skin was shiny with sweat.

“Can I come in?” I asked.

“Are you going to tell her she doesn’t have to go to the hospital?”

“I’m coming in.”
