“Yesterday she showed me that if you open your coat and stand on the ice, the wind will blow you across the yard.”

I frowned. “That sounds safe.”

“At our last home in Afghanistan, it was hardly safe to leave the house; I try to applaud when she stretches her wings, but it is terrifying to watch.”

“Maybe have her wear a bike helmet or something?”

“That’s a good idea.” As he turned a corner, the car lost traction for a second. “We’re good!”

“We’re good?” I reached for the car with my magic, but all I got was a headache as I clung to my seat. A moment later, the wheels caught, and we straightened out. I gently rotated my head on my neck, trying to ease the pounding.

“Four-wheel drive,” he said, his voice thick with tension.

I didn’t know much about four-wheel drive, but it seemed as useful as a bucket on the Titanic in this storm.

Realizing I was wringing my hands in my lap, I sat on them. To distract us both, I said, “Knock knock.”

Hassan glanced at me. “I say ‘Who’s there?’ Correct?”

“Yes.”

We went for another block when he turned to me again. “Oh, I’m sorry. Who’s there?”

I grinned. “Interrupting cow.”

“Then you say ‘Moo,’ right? Too late, Scharf already told me that one. She calls you a menace.” I couldn’t tell from his flat tone if he was joking or not.

“Forget it!” Even my jokes were backfiring. Now I knew something was seriously wrong.

“What are you two working on?” he asked at the next stoplight when he could examine my face more carefully, his eyes looking so old in his face.

“Murder downtown.”

Hassan nodded. “I heard about the riot. We still can’t do a drop-off at the arena. Nasty business. Your business?”

Sometimes I forgot that under his happy-go-lucky exterior, a combat veteran lurked. After Scharf’s deployment, her unit had helped arrange a visa for Hassan to come to the States with his family since the Taliban wanted everyone he’d ever interacted with dead.

“Um…”

When I didn’t continue, he shot me a look that said, Don’t screw with me, Orange.

“Half of it is my business. Or a third of it. Depending.”

He turned forward but kept an eye on me in the rearview mirror. “Explain.”

“Well, we have one totally normal murder victim, except the guy who did it is already locked up. And then we have one victim—who’s like me. And we have one potentially totally different dude, probably a dude, messing with the rain and everybody in DC, potentially turning them homicidal.”

He gaped at me and started when someone behind us honked. He put his foot on the gas. “And Scharf is out there without you?”

“Um…” I cleared my throat; his loyalty to Scharf was absolute. It extended to me only as a courtesy to her. “It’s not the kind of threat that I could help with. In fact, I am mostly making things worse.”

“That is not true.”

My phone erupted in an old-fashioned ringtone. The screen said, Astrid. “Sorry.” I hit the button to accept. “Are you okay?” I asked before she could say anything.

“Something’s wrong.”

The bottom fell out of my stomach. “Who else is dead?”

“Who else? Who first? That’s not a funny joke, Orange. The only thing that’s dead is these recipes!”

“What are you talking about?”

“The ingredients spoiled. I was selling them on campus, and people are asking for their money back.”

“What?”

“One guy almost hit me.”

“What?”

“Orange, take this seriously. We can’t sell these.”

I’d never baked anything bad in my life. Juya called it a part of my magic. People liked—no, loved—my food. Always. “I don’t understand.”

“They weren’t off. They were awful!”

The back of my neck itched as something occurred to me. “How close is Howard University to Chinatown?”

Astrid took a moment to answer. “Well, Chinatown is on H Street, right? Howard is on Euclid.”

I waited for more. “Yeah, that doesn’t help me.” Even after being in DC for two months, anything not connected to a crime scene or the main Metro stops eluded me.

Hassan murmured, “Twenty blocks north.”

“There are three alphabets worth of horizontal streets,” she explained. “Literally the letters A to W, then two-syllable words like ‘Euclid’ and ‘Fairmont,’ and then three-syllable words, mostly presidents.”

I thought about that for a second. “What?”

“Why does it matter? I’m trying to have a serious conversation about our business.”

I tried to get my heart rate under control. “Astrid, I’m so sorry about the recipes. I don’t know what’s wrong, but I’ll figure this out—I promise. For now, don’t sell anything I made. Throw it all away.” Who knew what kind of terrible magic I baked into those treats?

“You can’t give up when it doesn’t work!” she exclaimed.

“I know. But I can’t fix the ones you have, and I don’t want to spread them across the city if they’re not right.” There, that sounded professional.

“I got another rejection. Maybe I should stop.”

I sat up, razor straight. “Stop school?”

This was Astrid, the most driven person I knew, who plowed through obstacles like they were bowling pins.

“Maybe you’re right. Maybe this is crazy.”

“Ahhh, don’t make any hasty decisions today. Okay? Um, throw them away, that’s it.”

“I feel so powerless,” Astrid said. “We’re doing all this on faith.”

“And your business genius, and my baking skills. Faith is for suckers.”

She laughed. “There’s some proof of that. Optimists don’t get more opportunities; they notice more that are already there and pessimists don’t even see them.”

“See? Happy thoughts! Unicorns and rainbows! You hear me?”

There was a long pause that even Hassan seemed to hang on. “Yeah, I hear you,” Astrid finally said.

“Great! See you soon!” I hung up before she told me she was going to give up all worldly possessions and join a commune and have twenty kids, or something equally unlikely.

I turned to Hassan. “Please be careful, too. With the ice. And everything.”

“I am always careful,” he said with a grin.

“Seriously. It’s not normal ice. I’m not sure what’s going on yet.”

He sobered. “Another djinn?”

I shook my head, then stopped. “I don’t think so. This seems bigger than that.”

His mouth fell open. “What do you mean?”

“I mean, the djinni took out a city block. This storm covers three states.”

“Yikes.” He pronounced the word carefully.

“Just exercise caution, Hassan. I can’t be any more specific than that now, but I’ll tell you as soon as I know more.”

Hassan slowed, and I realize we were pulling up in front of Scharf’s clapboard house. Hers was the only free-standing structure on a long street of brick row houses in the cookie-cutter suburbs south of the Pentagon in Virginia.

“Orange…”

“Thanks a lot!” I jumped out and slumped when I saw Scharf on the front porch. “You called her.”

“Of course, I called her when you call me desperate for a ride without her!”

“Well, thanks anyway. I mean, just thanks for the ride.”

“Ma’a salāma. Although I don’t know why I tell you to go in peace. It seems like you and peace are like mayonnaise and water.”

“I think you mean oil and water.”

He cocked his head. “Isn’t mayonnaise oil?”

I opened my mouth and closed it. “Point.”

Scharf jogged down her sidewalk and ducked to speak to Hassan. “There’s another position available.”

He rolled his eyes.

“They keep asking,” she added.

“Would you vouch for these men?” he asked. I wasn’t sure what they were talking about.

Scharf said nothing.

“I am done being the mouthpiece for messages I cannot influence.”

Scharf nodded once. “I said I’d ask.”

She stood back, but he leaned towards the passenger seat. “When my wife has completed her degree, and I do not have to watch the children, I will look at another position. If they are worthy.”

“I’ll tell them,” Scharf said but didn’t move. She looked at me.

“I’m going.” I flipped up the collar of my orange leather coat against the continuing miserable drizzle and walked away as Scharf got in the car to chat more.

As I walked, I scanned the street carefully for anything red. It was tough with all the red brick. If the Lubber Fiend was lying in wait, he had a lot of camouflage.

Nothing moved except the dripping water, and I finally stumbled onto Scharf’s porch and tried the door handle. It didn’t move. She’d installed an automatic lock with a keypad on her front door after ghuls had destroyed it, so if it shut, it was locked.

I turned back to see her and Hassan in an animated conversation in the car. Scharf would know something was up if I asked her to open it for me. She hadn’t bothered to give me the code since I could open it with a murmur of magic. Most of the time.

I leaned against the door.

“Invader!”

I tried to spin around to defend myself. It took me almost five seconds. I looked around, hands out, my only weapons. A tiny shape less than a foot tall jumped onto the porch.

He farted and said in his high-pitched squeak, “You woke me up!”

I crouched to peer at the gnome. “Oh, thank God, Officer Grimm. Can you open the door?”

Scharf hadn’t bothered to name him; I’d called him Grimm in a fit of humor that seemed grotesque now. He’d been a gag gift from one of her friends: a garden gnome in a police uniform. Neither had known he was real. The gnome looked from me to the door.

When he didn’t answer, I said, “Please open the door?”

“Why?”

“I’ve temporarily lost my magic, okay? Open the door!”

“What will you give me for it?” He’d learned quickly about bargaining by watching me. I wrinkled my nose and wanted to offer him a cookie, but I didn’t trust my cooking right now.

Unable to think of anything, I finally said, “My thanks.”

He glowed and said, “Good bargain, goblin.”

“Okay?” I replied, befuddled and slightly sheepish. He jumped off the porch and dove for the ground. It parted for him like water. He and I had similar earth powers. We were probably distant cousins in the magical creature universe.

A minute later, the lock clicked, and I opened the door to find him dangling from the knob. He tumbled to the floor, bowed, and tapped his foot.

“Thank you, noble gnome.”

He farted again and saluted, wearing a proud grin, before turning to disappear into the living room.

“Wait!”

With all the magic in the air and the Lubber Fiend circling, I wanted to check on my box, my literal inheritance from my father, but I’d buried it beneath the house after a ghul attack and I didn’t have any magic to dig it up.

“Can you get me my box? From the basement?”

“What will—”

“What will I give you? I know, I know.”

“Cookie!”

I sighed. Apparently, thanks only went so far; we really were related. But since I was now on the same side of the door as the kitchen, I could handle that. “Okay, deal.”

There was no chime in my head. I swallowed and quelled a spurt of panic; I really had burnt myself out. It’s coming back. Of course, it’s coming back.

Fortunately, I didn’t need magic to bake a cookie.

Grimm dove into the living room floor and through to the crawlspace beneath, carving another hole in the hardwood. I winced but couldn’t do anything about it now.

I took off my jacket, and let it drop in the entranceway. Then I stared at my jacket on the floor, horrified.

Slowly, I bent, picked it up, and hung it in the closet next to the front door. I was a goblin; we kept house. Scharf was the slob.

Something is seriously wrong.

I looked around her home with fresh eyes. I’d done a massive cleanup a few weeks ago, and we’d slowly been working out a level of clean that was acceptable to both of us. She had confessed that it was nicer to walk around her living room rather than crawl over and under sports equipment, laundry from the last month, and mail from the last six.

In the last week, without my having realized it, the junk had crept back. The living room was still livable, with my belongings stacked by the couch under the window where I slept and Scharf’s gym stuff in some kind of order on the opposite side of the room, but it did not look like a goblin lived here. I grabbed for a kettlebell on its side in the middle of the beige carpet but couldn’t budge it. My magic was genuinely gone. I couldn’t even summon enough of it to pick up that small piece of earth and move it to its rack. I wasn’t strong enough to lift it without magic.

Cookies. The next step was cookies, which was not a bad idea under any circumstances. I abandoned the mess to walk across the hall.

Scharf’s galley kitchen had just enough room for a round table and a couple of chairs at the end of the dual counters. The stove was electric and temperamental, but homeless hobgoblins couldn’t be choosers.

A couple of baggies with old mug cakes lay on the counter next to the breadbox. I approached them warily. Astrid wasn’t wrong; the liquid was lumpy, and the flour seemed to be half the volume I put in and slightly gray. I couldn’t resist shaking the baggies and saying, “What’s wrong with you?”

They didn’t answer. I dashed them into the trash; at human strength, I didn’t even break any of the bags.

I wrenched open the cupboard with my ingredients and eyed the chocolate chips, but realized I wanted to make something else. I’d bake something I loved, a recipe that had never failed me. “Take that, anonymous forces of evil!”

I gathered flour, sugar, and baking powder. Next, I pulled out a bowl from another cabinet where Scharf stored her blender and her smoothie ingredients, then dug in the fridge for an orange. This was one of the first recipes I’d ever learned to make.

There was a bakery in my hometown in California where my mom and I often went since she didn’t have the slightest twinge of domesticity. One morning shortly after I’d learned to read my name, I saw a sign that said, Orange Scones inside the glass counter. At the top of my small voice, I announced they were mine. Fortunately, the baker had found me charming rather than obnoxious when I insisted that he give them all to me because they had my name on them.

Oh, for the days when I could claim the world by writing my name on it in black marker, I thought, trying for a laugh as I grated the rind for zest. The baker had taught me the recipe when I started working there at age sixteen.

I stirred milk and eggs into the dry ingredients. The quiet scrape of the spoon along the bowl and the slow magic that transformed milk and flour into batter hypnotized me. Most of the stress of the day drained out of my muscles as the tempting smell of butter and oranges wafted through the kitchen. I peeled the orange and squeezed the juice into the batter, adding a few chunks of pulp, then I formed scones and put them on a tray.

I stuck them in the oven and collapsed onto the tile floor, letting the warmth of the oven door seep into my bones. Closing my eyes, I contemplated putting on some music but didn’t trust the radio. Instead, I hummed an old jig my father had sung as he teased my mother in the kitchen. “The queen is in her kitchen and the king has to fart. One rules a kingdom and the other rules his heart.”

My phone rang, and I juggled to see the front and realized it was an alarm. Every day at two on the West coast, I called my mom. It was her best time of day. She’d have lunch but wouldn’t have dealt yet with the discombobulation of “group activities.”

I braced myself, pasted a smile on my face, and hit the number.

“Hello,” a woman said. “Good Rest Home, Sharon Sinclair’s room.”

I frowned at the phone. “Who is this?”

“Who is this?”

“This is her daughter, Orange.”

“Oh, Orange. This is her nurse, Harriet.”

“Could you put her on the phone? I always call at two.”

“I’m afraid not.”

“What?” A few really weird things had happened. A goblin had died; my recipes had failed, and now the torture of saying hello to my mother who didn’t remember me would not happen? “Why not?”

“She grew extremely agitated after your last call. It’s affecting her recovery.”

“What are you talking about? She isn’t recovering.”

“She’s making excellent progress.”

“You mean she hasn’t forgotten an entire food group between this week and last?”

A brief silence followed. Yep, this aid was new. They burned through home aids monthly, and it always took them a second to get used to me. “Well, that is to say, there is no further deterioration in her condition. Her memory remains stable.”

“Stably gone?”

Her voice was prim as she said, “You could call her doctor to discuss this. Maybe we can try again in a few weeks.”

I couldn’t believe this. “You’re genuinely not going to let me talk to my mother?”

“Orange, we all want what’s best for her.”

“I don’t understand what’s different about today.”

“The doctor will speak with you tomorrow, okay?” When I didn’t respond, Harriet said, “You have a good day now.”

She ended the call. With difficulty, I resisted throwing the phone across the room. Instead, I tucked it in my pocket, then threw a spoon across the room. It clattered to the floor and took the pile of Washington Posts on the table with it. The avalanche sounded like music.

“Hey!” Scharf dodged the newspapers as she stepped into the tiny space.

“Sorry.” I rolled my neck. “You shouldn’t sneak up on people like that.”

She laughed, but it wasn’t a cheerful sound. Now that I could get a proper look at her in the kitchen's light, I was shocked.

She looked like someone had punched her twice, producing pronounced dark circles around her eyes. She wore the bottom half of a suit and an untucked white button-down shirt. Her hair was still wet from the perpetual storm.

She sat down opposite me on the floor against a cabinet. “What smells good?”

“Orange scones.” I glanced at the oven. “They do smell good, right?”

“Yeah. What’s in them?”

The scent of citrus and caramelizing sugar infused the air. “Um, oranges?”

“Oh.” She peered into the oven for a second. “I thought you meant they were your recipe.”

I smiled, thinking again about my hometown bakery. “It’s from the man who taught me to bake. They’re my favorite.”

“They smell great.”

“Thanks.”

Unwilling to meet her eyes, I picked out a stray thread in the seam of my jeans. As our last conversation scrolled its way through my head, I winced at some things we’d said.

“I didn’t for one second think you killed him,” Scharf said, running a hand through her disheveled hair.

I nodded. “I didn’t think you thought I killed him.”

“It’s just—there are procedures to this, and I can’t ignore the fact that you knew the victim.”

My anger sparked a little, but I took a deep breath and said, “I get it.”

This was Scharf I was talking to. Of course, she was going to be upset when the rules got twisted around. I slowly stood up and pulled out the scones. I looked around for plates but didn’t want to break the spell, the first kind words we’d exchanged in days. I dropped an oven mitt on the floor and lay the pan of scones on it. Then I plopped next to them and leaned against the oven door again, savoring the warmth.

As an experiment, I used magic to pull a scone off the baking tray, and it obediently rose into the air. I could feel the scone the way I felt my foot. When I stretched out this weird sense, I could also feel the house and the stones beneath it. I blew out a breath in relief.

Scharf grinned. “Thanks.”

She snatched it out of the air and tossed it from hand to hand to let it cool off a bit.

My goblin half and my human half had bargained in a moment of desperation: my human life for my goblin power. I had thought that meant I’d go on my merry way with access to my magic anytime I wanted. But I now knew the bargain’s definition of living was a little narrower than mine. It did not include nearly killing myself investigating crimes, but it included my favorite scones. Good to know.

I tried to lift a second scone for me, and it lifted a few inches before it toppled. Baking one tray of scones, doing something I loved, had allowed me to gain a teeny piece of earth magic.

I pulled it off the tray with my fingers, wincing when my pinkie brushed the burning tray; I didn’t have my magic to harden my skin against the heat. Sighing, I sank into the oven for my first blissful bite. My headache cleared as the sugar hit my stomach.

As I ate, I felt more power return. My magic sorted out the mush in my brain, my stiff right shoulder I hadn’t noticed in the ache of my head, and a dull throb deep in my tailbone. I hadn’t had my powers long, but I’d forgotten how fragile humans are. How did Scharf march into danger knowing this and worse was possible? I’d only slipped and fallen.

Scharf mirrored my bite and closed her eyes. “This is a sin.”

I smirked. “Your last confession was a decade ago.”

“Probably two. But seriously, so good. Thank you, Orange.”

I nodded and finished mine.

“Speaking of sin…” she said.

“Speaking of sin?” I gaped. “What follows that non sequitur?”

“Demon?”

I shook my head. “Not yet time.”

“It’s a bombshell.”

I straightened. “So goblins you could get behind without trouble, but demons are your line in the sand?”

“Eighteen years of church and confession and Sunday school. You have to admit, it’s a little different.”

I shrugged. “Not that different.”

“It’s just…”

I held up a hand. “Let’s solve this case. And then I promise you can grill me.”

After we had eaten a scone each, Scharf leaned over and stretched her arm toward my face. I backed up an inch or two. “What are you doing?”

She pulled her hand away and said, “May I?”

“May you what?”

“Your ears.”

My breath left my body in a whoosh. Yes, I kept them covered and glamoured from everyone, but this was Scharf. I wasn’t sure why I was hesitating. She’d seen the bigger, more destructive pieces of my magic. What was a little extra flap of skin?

Vanity, thy name is goblin. With another sigh, I dropped my illusions.

She jerked a bit. “I forgot how—”

“Orange I am?”

“You look better without it.”

Without the illusions, my skin darkened to a citrus hue; my hair got bushier and pinker; my eyes grew larger; my nails sharpened, and my ears…

I shook my head. “You sound like my mom. ‘Be proud of yourself,’” I mimicked, pushing down the ache. I would have given anything to hear that from her again.

I took another breath and pulled my hair back from the right side of my head and turned to the side. “Of course, it might have been helpful to know that I should be proud of being a goblin.”

Scharf ignored me and touched my earlobe. I fought the impulse to pull away. Most goblin ears extended four inches, but mine grew only an extra inch, forming a floppy point like a drunken elf’s ear. The outside edge was ragged.

Kneeling to get a closer look, she pulled the tip taut, gently stretching it away from my body.

“Wow.”

I felt like I was sitting there naked and jerked away from her. I covered my ear again, tucking the extra skin back behind the lobe.

She wore a gentle smile. “Amazing.”

“Really? They kind of gross me out.”

“You’re a goblin.”

“You’ve seen me pull down buildings with my brain. It’s not like this is the strangest thing about me.”

“It’s… real.” She grabbed another scone.

I took a bite myself. “How are you so okay about any of this? From the first time I met you and you shot a chimera between the eyes without even blinking, weird stuff has happened, and you say, ‘Oh, okay.’”

“I’m just screaming on the inside.”

I swallowed. “Scharf. You’re the most practical person I know. Except that can’t be true, given everything we’ve done.”

She cleared her throat. “I think you’re right. I am practical; I try not to let my expectations of a case blind me to reality. We’re in trouble with this case because a lot of agents are falling into that trap. You saw how they saw the body and, without a shred of investigation, were so certain of what happened.” She gestured to my ears. “The evidence is incontrovertible. Plus, something really strange happened one night when I was in the Academy.”

“What happened?”

She squinted. “I’ll tell you someday. Your walking into my life wasn’t the first impossible moment of my life.”

“It’s just demons that give you the squick.”

She opened her mouth.

“Sorry I brought it up.”

She closed her mouth. Still feeling raw after dropping my glamour, I found I couldn’t meet her eyes. I focused and put it back in place, ignoring her flinch.

To have something else to look at, I took out my phone. I swiped my music app open, my stomach knotting up again. Nothing that played music had worked for more than a day. I hit “shuffle” and waited. The music skipped through two songs, and my heart sank, but finally, an old Black Eyed Peas song sputtered to life.

Scharf burst out laughing, a genuine laugh that crinkled the corners of her dark eyes. She finished the scone and gestured for another. I closed my eyes and pulled another scone up for her from the pan on the floor. She caught it with a nod.

A crack sounded outside, and we both flinched. It was a sound we were growing used to, the death of tree branches and sometimes entire trees or houses under the weight of the ice. But here in these eight feet of kitchen, Scharf and I had repaired the heart of our weird little home and our tenuous partnership.

I closed my eyes for a second.

“Orange!”

“What?” I opened my eyes to find myself almost on the floor.

“You almost face-planted into a hot tray.”

“I have to get some sleep. Eventually.”

She helped me sit up again and asked, “When did you sleep last?”

I tried to count. “Uh, the day before yesterday, maybe?”

“That’s a form of torture, you know. You can’t do that to yourself.”

I was about to protest that I was a goblin, and I could push myself much harder than most people, but then I remembered the horrifying moment when I’d reached for my magic and hadn’t found it and nodded. “Yeah. I just have to check my box and I’ll rest. The gnome is getting it.”

“So your magic is in the box?”

“No!” I thought about that one. “I don’t know…”

She bit her lip to hide a smile. “You still haven’t opened it.”

I shook my head. Might as well get all the humiliation out of the way. “My challengers seem to believe I got it open, which seems to be all that matters.”

“Obviously not, since they’re still attacking you!” She took a breath and leaned back. “And I can’t believe I’m just casually talking about assault and battery.”

I ran a thumb over my knuckles and shuddered. “They don’t want the box. They want to make sure I’m worthy of the box? Or of them?” I shook my head. “Apparently when goblins get a new leader, instead of telling her where the stapler is or even just saying hi, they have to challenge her to make sure she’s worthy. It’s really fun.”

“Cookie now, please!” The gnome said from the doorway, holding my box, which was almost as big as he was.

I offered the tray of scones, but he wrenched the box away. “That’s not a cookie!”

I groaned.

Scharf reached for the box. “Give me that.”

“Nooooo!” he said, hopping from foot to foot.

“I’ll find a cookie!” I said and rolled to my feet to dig through my stash.

“Ha!” Scharf said, and I turned to see the box in her hands with the gnome dangling from it.

“Your bargain is forfeit!” the gnome squealed.

“I know, I know,” I said and opened another cupboard. “Do you have a cookie?”

I turned when Scharf said nothing. She’d gone three shades whiter. “Orange.”

“What happened?”

“I was grabbing it…”

“What happened?”

“Cookie, goblin!” the gnome said, breathless from holding on.

I grabbed it from Scharf, shaking it so the gnome fell to the floor. I looked it over frantically, from the letter taped to the bottom to the intricate carvings on the top and sides. It was the size of a breadbox but flatter. “It’s fine. It’s here and it’s fine. What’s the problem?”

Scharf reached out and caressed the right side of the box. I heard a click, and the blood drained from my face. She turned the box sideways. One carving of what looked like stairs marching from the bottom of the box to the lid sank into the wood. It was some kind of button.

“Oh, my God.”

Scharf laughed.

“How did you do that?” I asked.

“I pressed it. Accidentally.”

“I want a cookie!”

Scharf opened the cabinet with her smoothie ingredients, dug behind them, and came up with a package of store-bought sandwich cookie knockoffs.

I gasped. “You have store-bought cookies in this house?”

She flicked one to him and he toppled to the ground and stuffed it in his mouth.

“Save me from goblins,” Scharf said as she put them back.

“I am a gnome,” the gnome said solemnly. “The bargain has been fulfilled.”

“And gnomes,” Scharf added. “I’ve had those for years, okay? They’re probably expired. They belonged to my ex.”

I gasped again. “You fed him a two-year-old, store-bought cookie?!”

Scharf looked at the gnome splayed below us. The cookie looked gigantic sticking out of his tiny mouth. “He likes it. Do you want to try opening that now?”

I’d forgotten the box in my hands. I shook my head and pressed the lowest stair and then the next. One after the other, they sank into the wood. Nothing happened.

I dropped to the ground and pressed every bit of carving I could get my hands on. “They’re buttons. That’s why it hasn’t opened.”

Scharf arched one eyebrow. “That’s good, right?”

Unfortunately, I couldn’t get any of the others to depress. The whole lid and every side were full of carvings, abstract swirls, a cat, a bird, a dog, and a man and woman. And stairs. The two people were dressed in what looked like Victorian-era clothes with a bustle on her dress and breeches on him. None of it budged.

I went back to the stairs.

Click, click, click, click. I tugged on the lid, but nothing happened.

“Orange, relax!”

“It won’t open.”

“Orange, the other side has stairs!”

When she turned the box, I saw another set of stairs, a mirror image of the ones on the right. When I pressed them, nothing happened until I clicked the top one and then the one below. You had to go up one set of stairs and down the other. I tried the lid again, and it moved a centimeter.

I put the box down, so I had more fingers to press at carvings. “Help me!”

I crouched so I could get my eye right up to the crack. I couldn’t see into the black interior.

When I shook the box, it gurgled. “What the…”

A wave of water spat out and drenched my face and the floor. And it just kept coming.

Scharf dove for it.

“Where are you going? That’s mine!”

Gushing water out of the three sides, the box rattled in her hands as she threw it into the sink.

I scrambled past her and stuck the stopper in the drain.

“Good idea,” she said, and we watched as the flow slowed down and stopped.

I grabbed a cup from the sink and dipped it into the water to hold it up to the light, thinking, This is my legacy?

I was about to toss it back in the sink, but then something weird happened.

The water changed. Or I changed. I felt it in the glass. Like I felt the earth. I pushed on it like I would manipulate earth.

Nothing happened.

“What are you doing?” Scharf asked.

I blinked, reaching for the water in the sink with my weird sense of magic. Was it magic? I’d never been able to manipulate water before.

“Orange?”

I sagged against the counter, exhausted again. Impossibly more tired than when I’d come home.

Scharf’s hand touched my shoulder blade. “You okay?”

I straightened and forced nonchalance into my voice as I said, “Well, now we know what’s in the box.”

I crouched and carefully tried to open the lid again. It only budged a centimeter again, and no other water came out.

I wiped the water off my face and wrung out my hair.

The pitter-patter of little feet jump-started my pulse until I remembered the gnome. He skidded to a stop under the table.

“It’s raining, raining on my home,” he crooned, scowling. “Bargain breaker.”

He dove for the floor, cracking the floorboards.

“Stop that!” Scharf dove for him and got a hand around his ankle before he made it to the crawlspace beneath her house.

“Maybe we should get him a trapdoor?”

I took a hesitant sip from the glass. The water was cold and clear, but I caught the slightest hint of moss or something wild in it. I sighed. “Thanks, Dad.”

Halfheartedly, I pushed some more spots on the box, but if every bit of the carvings was a button, I could push them in different orders for my entire life and not find the right combination. Reluctantly, I handed it back to the gnome.

“Can you put it back?”

“What will you—”

Without answering, I went to the smoothie cupboard, pulled out the ancient cookies, and thrust the entire package at him.

He held the cookies and the box, his eyes as round as two coins in his head. “It’s too much!”

“It really, really isn’t,” I retorted.

He bent for the new hole, and I turned him towards the door. “Not that way!”

He farted and harrumphed and rolled away, back to his wet kingdom. One day, the entire floor of Scharf’s house was going to fall through. First ghuls had burned through it, then the gnome punched through it, and now we’d flooded it.

I looked at the water in the sink and carefully put the glass in my cupboard, not quite willing to let it wash down the drain.

Scharf sat wearily at the dining room table, sidestepping the pile of newspapers like they didn’t exist. I retrieved the scones that had fortunately escaped the splash zone, and sat across from her, liberating the opposite white chair from her breakfast dishes. I had let things go around here.

“You should take a nap,” she said.

“I’m fine.” The adrenaline of getting my box open had me wide awake, which had other worries asserting themselves. “What happened after I left the crime scene?”

“The medical examiner took the body away.”

And now I could worry about a doctor cutting open a goblin somewhere. I sat on my hands to stop myself from asking her what they’d found.

“I finished the interviews we could do.” Scharf ate another scone. “One other woman is living in the building on the third floor, but she was at the parade. And I met with Kui Liu, who works in that music shop, Liu Imports.”

I perked up.

She shook her head. “He’s a thousand years old and has one leg. It wasn’t him.”

“What about Joe of Pasta Joe’s?”

She considered for half a second. “No, his alibi is airtight. Who would have known he hangs out with federal judges? And he knew nothing about the Restaurant Killer, and it’s going down as another of his victims. According to BAU and my boss and everyone else.”

“But—”

“But the Restaurant Killer is locked up. So no, I don’t have a lead or suspect, except the one in maximum security, and no next steps because BAU took over the case.” She sighed. “I’m liaising with my own agency.”

“But you love liaising.”

“No, I like when others have to liaise with me.”

“Right.”

She beat her hands against her legs. She was usually never still, and it warmed my heart further to see her fidget. I hadn’t realized until now how worn she had seemed.

She laced her hands together again. “We caught the killer.”

“What? You just said…”

“No, I don’t mean we caught this killer. We haven’t. I mean the Restaurant Killer is in prison.”

I agreed that whoever had killed Rodger and the goblin wasn’t her long-ago serial killer if even they were the same person, but I couldn’t understand why she was so sure. “So you’re saying the man in jail killed the people you said he did?”

“That investigation was solid. He was convicted without a shadow of a doubt. We didn’t screw that up.”

“So who killed Rodger?”

She didn’t answer. I ate another scone, and my phone skipped through three more songs before it tried to play Gregorian chants my mother had downloaded for me several years ago. The chanters were severely out of tune. I turned it off, and the world felt a little colder again.

She straightened. “I keep harping on the manual strangulation, the pattern of bruising with the thumbs against the windpipe. We never let that part of it get out. And the fork was in the right eye.”

“He had a fifty-fifty shot.”

“It all adds up. There were deeper similarities only the killer knew.”

I shrugged. “The killer and you.”

Her brow knitted. “What?”

“You said only the killer knew about all that. You and the killer knew. And the original investigators.”

“It’s not my first choice to go accusing my own people.”

“Fuller knew best of all,” I pushed.

“I know that.”

I sat up. “And he was in the neighborhood that night. Remember, he was at the game?”

She looked at the ceiling for an answer. “The Restaurant Killer made his name. Why would he call that into question?”

I considered grabbing another scone, but we’d already cleaned up half the tray, and my stomach protested. “Yeah, but he loves this: being back in action, having everyone jump to his tune. You certainly jumped high enough.”

“What does that mean?”

“Nothing!” I stuffed a scone into my mouth.

“Where is the motive? Why do it?”

I swallowed and almost choked. “Either he was wrong back then, or he’s wrong now.”

“Let’s ask him.” Scharf started out of the kitchen.

“Whoa! You want to ask an FBI agent if he killed somebody?”

“Not him, Hastings.”

I shuddered. “The serial killer?”

She stood half in and out of the kitchen, hands on her hips. “Did we get the right guy or not? One bargain, and we’ll know for sure. Come on.”

“But—”

12

“They’re not locking you up,” Scharf said, her eyes on me as I stared at the razor wire and squat cement building with a sign that read Chesapeake Bay Maximum Security Prison.

“It could have been,” I said, still not moving. They’d arrested me and interrogated me when they suspected me of a plot to assassinate the president.

“It wasn’t you,” Scharf said, starting towards the towering walls.

“It could have been!” I repeated.

“If you dwell on the bullets that miss you, you’re letting them hit.”

I scrambled after her, walking gingerly on the ice, and murmured, “Only you could spout philosophy with bullets.”

“What?”

“Nothing.”

At the first gate, she unclipped her Glock from her belt and handed it to the guy behind the window. “We’re here to see John Hastings.”

Heart pounding, I held up my driver’s license. We were trying out a new trick since Scharf said an FBI agent would never interview a suspect alone so I couldn’t go invisible.

I transformed my driver's license into an FBI badge with the same glamour I used to adjust my appearance. Although I could easily make myself look like a normal human being without orange-tinted skin or floppy ears, it was way, way, way harder to glamour objects. As the guard read off my name, I prayed he wouldn’t reach for the license. If my fingers left it, the game would be up.

Fortunately, he didn’t seem that worried about people trying to get into prison, and another guard soon escorted us into the first of a series of barred enclosures. The gate slamming behind us left me gasping for air, but several orange lines sprouted in various directions, which was reassuring. I could get out of here anytime.

The itch down my spine grew as we tunneled deeper into the facility and there were fewer and fewer orange lines showing me the way out. We went through a series of gates and doors that all locked behind us.

I’d only spent a few days locked up during my first case with Scharf, but it left me with an allergy to beige rooms and jail cells. Finally, the guard escorted us to a room with a metal table and three chairs. The walls were gray, not beige, but it didn’t make me feel better.

It felt like a small animal was trying to gnaw through my guts. I leaned toward Scharf and whispered, “Where’s the glass?”

“What glass?”

“The glass between the murderer and us? Dramatic drawings on the walls? The overhead bulb?”

“This isn’t Silence of the Lambs. This is where everyone meets with any prisoner in this place.”

I sighed as we sat down. “So, we’re actually less safe than Clarice was.”

Scharf smirked. “Aside from the obstacle course at the beginning, I don’t think you should draw procedural tips from that film.”

“You’ve been on that obstacle course?”

She nodded. “Yeah. Great workout.”

I threw up my shaky hands, then quickly hid them under the table as the door buzzed. It seemed as loud as a gunshot; I flinched as it clicked open.

“Sit up straight,” Scharf said. “Try to look official.”

Somehow, I didn’t think the curve in my spine would help with how official I seemed, but I ratcheted my shoulders back.

As the door swung open, I took a deep breath and tried to match Scharf’s fierce look.

The man who walked in wearing handcuffs and an obnoxious shade of orange (which was really saying something coming from somebody with my skin tone) was a definite anticlimax.

I was going to make a crack about Anthony Hopkins but didn’t want to draw any attention to myself. He was middle-aged, middling height, balding, out of shape, and pasty. This man had killed half a dozen people? Terrorized restaurant owners throughout Baltimore? Maybe Scharf was right, and the actual killer was out there all this time.

As Hastings took a seat across from us, it thrilled me to see the guard leave the shackles around his wrists and back up to stand against one wall.

I tried to remind myself that I was a very powerful magical creature, the leader of all goblins, but my right leg jiggled under the table.

The man looked between the two of us with absolutely no expression and said in a light tenor that matched his body but not his reputation, “To what do I owe the pleasure?”

Scharf glanced at me, and I gulped. She had a very annoying habit of letting me lead in interviews, so I could ask whatever ridiculous questions I had without diminishing her authority. Instead of the usual good cop/bad cop, we played random cop/silent cop.

I said, “FBI.”

I didn’t elaborate, not wanting to admit that I wasn’t personally with the FBI, while also hoping he would draw the right conclusions.

“XYZ,” Hastings said, and I frowned. Scharf wore a much better poker face; only one eyebrow twitched. “Forgive me,” he continued. “I thought we were reciting random letters of the alphabet.”

Scharf said, “I’m an agent with the Federal Bureau of Investigation.”

He smiled, looking a little like a church deacon, before he said, “The real killer has struck.”

I gasped, then bit my tongue to keep quiet.

This time Scharf didn’t twitch at all. “A jury of your peers convicted you in a court of law. First-degree murder. Six victims. The evidence was incontrovertible.”

Hastings traced an idle figure eight on the table in front of him. “And yet here you are. Should I contact my lawyer?”

Scharf shrugged. “That’s your right. But we aren’t here about your current convictions.”

Then she looked at me. I almost jammed a nail into my palm, trying to get my hands still as I said, “I’ll make a deal with you.”

“You mean I will not live out my life behind these bars, and you will repeal my miscarriage of justice? For that, I’ll definitely need my lawyer.”

I shook my head. “Not that kind of deal.”

“What have you got?” he said, sitting back. As I glanced at Scharf, he said, “Yes, check in with your keeper. Why am I talking to her since you’re clearly the alpha?”

To my shock, Scharf smiled. “She has more clout than either of us combined. I’ve just had more experience with scum.”

That seemed to make Hastings happy, and after a moment, Scharf sighed. She’d lost points with that last crack in a game I hadn’t realized we were playing, and I hadn’t gotten a copy of the rule book.

I was feeling less scared, but infinitely warier. Appearances could be deceiving. I’d braced myself for somebody crazy, out of control, rabid. But Hastings was observant and charismatic. I suppose you had to be if you could get away with murder six times over before finally getting caught. If he stuck out, somebody would’ve noticed.

I’d read more of Monster in the Kitchen, Fuller’s aggrandizing book about how the FBI had caught him. Hastings had been connected to the first man he’d killed. After the manager at East Side Eggs, where he had worked for six weeks, had called him stupid and shady one too many times, Hastings had snapped and strangled him, leaving a sliver of a fingernail behind and a fork in his eye.

It was a matter of connecting the murders from that point since the first murder was considerably less tidy than the others. It took a solid five years of work to build the case.

Hastings stared at me with vacant blue eyes. “Do you have the standing to deal with me?”

Frowning, I asked, “What do you mean?”

“You said you’d make a deal, but what do you have that I might want? And if you have something, do you have the standing, the power, to grant it? I’m guessing the answer is no. On both counts.” I had to hand it to him, he had a better understanding of bargains than most goblins.

Scharf’s eyes sharpened. “You guess?”

“I know.”

He was right. Bargains had to balance, and both sides had to deliver on their promises. Scharf and I desperately wanted an answer from him, an answer he’d never given in hours of interrogation and months of trial. What could we offer him to balance that?

I froze, and the hairs on the back of my neck stood at attention. Hastings didn’t have to admit anything. With my magic, I only needed him to deny his involvement in the murders once we had a deal in place. To me, a denial would be as good as a confession for learning the truth. With a bargain, I’d know if he was lying.

I relaxed, feigning confidence, keeping my hands well below the table, and said, “Make me an offer. You deny it—”

He grinned and matched my posture, an attempt to sync with me that was too calculated to be anything but creepy. The longer I spoke with this guy, the more off he seemed. “But you can have that for free. I didn’t commit the crimes for which I’ve been so wrongly convicted.”

Cursing inwardly, I tried to keep the disappointment off my face. It was no good if he gave it to me for free.

Scharf kept her breathing steady as she said, “Short of getting out, you want for absolutely nothing here? You’re perfectly happy with your life in prison?”

“I’m perfectly happy,” he said in a monotone, but I could sense the bitter anger behind his words.

“There’s nothing you need or want from anyone?”

He shrugged. “Nothing.”

I was catching on fast. “Yet you took this meeting.”

“Yes, I did.”

“Perfectly happy people don’t leave their perfectly happy existence to chat with strangers.”

“I’ll admit to a certain level of curiosity,” he said, the small smile gone from his lips.

“Come on, admit it. It must be boring in here.” I leaned in closer, my hands clenched together, my heartbeat fluttering. “What did we drag you out of?”

Hastings ran a hand over his balding head. “My cell. But you knew that.”

“Day after day with the same view, the same guards, limited conversation. I promise to amuse you if you deny the charges once more. Do we have a deal?”

“Ambitious. I’m intrigued.” His flat eyes met mine. “But not amused.”

“Say, ‘Deal.’”

“Who are you? Monty Hall? Do you expect me to name the price of your eye-catching leather jacket?”

Scharf made a subtle gesture under the table for me to keep him talking. At least, I thought that was what her twirling finger meant.

This was a risk. What would amuse a serial killer?

I said nothing. Finally, he burst out laughing, which had the guard’s fingers twitching in the corner. “Oh, we have a deal,” Hastings said. “I haven’t been this intrigued in four years, six months, twelve days, and six hours. Since the farce of the trial I was subjected to.”

“Sure,” I said in a spurt of annoyance. “They shoved a fork in their own eye.”

“Drunks are so clumsy. Such regrettable examples of humanity, one might even say inhuman.”

I froze when he said inhuman, hanging onto an even expression only by forcing power into my glamour. Nobody said anything.

He inhaled and tapped one fingertip on the counter. “But do… Do tell me your plans for my amusement.”

“You first,” I said.

“I beg your pardon?” he said, and I stayed silent until he shifted in his seat. “Fine, I didn’t commit the murders. I have to admit, though, that I’m curious why you’re so intent on hearing me say it yet again.”

I waited for the chime in my head, half hopeful we were already amusing him, but nothing. Clueless, I surveyed the room. What on earth would amuse him? Why did I agree to such a thing?

I stared at Scharf, who didn’t take her eyes off Hastings.

Suddenly, I got an idea. I knew something I could show him that was pretty unusual. Without preamble, I dropped my glamour. My skin tint turned darker. My nails elongated. My ears flopped. My eyes grew larger, and my teeth sharpened.

He blinked at me and tensed. Again, I got the impression of bottled fury before he smoothed his body language. “Neat trick. I’ll admit that I’m more than a little disgusted. But not amused.”

I very carefully told him, but not the guard or Scharf, not to notice me. When I went invisible, he jerked in his chair, but his expression never changed.

I faded into view, this time trying to exaggerate my features. I had a picture in my mind of my father, with his deep orange skin and elongated ears, and sharpened incisors. Hastings leaned forward, his eyes round. “Horrifying but not amusing. Is this an illusion? A mirror? Psychotropic drugs slipped into my food? A puzzle I’ll have to puzzle out more.”

I stuck my tongue out at him, and he leaped to his feet, the chair toppling behind him. He slammed his cuffed hands onto the table and barked at Scharf, “Your creature won’t disrespect me.”

Scharf’s hand shot to her belt, where her gun normally rested, while her other arm flew across my chest as if she could protect me. I gripped her wrist, intent on pushing her behind me when the guard reached him and pushed him into his chair.

Hastings swung around to face me, and I met his gaze. I knew enough about challenges not to look down.

“I believe I’ll eventually be amused,” he said, looking at me with keen eyes. “You’ve given me a lifetime of assumptions to reevaluate. I have believed one thing about the world.” Finally, he looked down and examined his nails. Did that mean I’d won the staring contest? I didn’t know. “I was incorrect. There is so much more inhumanity in the world to deal with.”

As he faked toward me again, I leaped behind the chair to get away. Scharf said, “We’re done.”

13

I heard nothing. No chime. Only deafening silence as the guard led Hastings from the room. I’d bargained his amusement for the truth. I hadn’t gotten it.

As I pounded the table in frustration, he laughed heartily, and the door closed. Scharf stood up. “Come on, Orange. Let’s go.”

I stared at the closed door in shock. I’d never failed at my end of a bargain. What was wrong with the world? When a door clanged somewhere deep in the building, I lunged to my feet. “Let’s go.”

I jogged through the exit protocols, pulling her along. Fresh air couldn’t come soon enough.

When we reached the final gate, I had to hold back from using magic to wrench it open as a guard let us out. I burst out into the rain. I paced and told people not to notice me, disappointed in myself and furious.

Scharf rolled her neck and her shoulders and kept one hand gripped around the base of her gun like she’d missed it. “Well?”

I scuffed one shoe against the pavement. “He’s crazy.”

“A psychiatrist examined Hastings—two of them. I believe the diagnosis was sociopath. Competent to stand trial, though, thankfully.”

I growled and kept pacing.

“Orange, calm down. You got him to make a deal! You did it.”

I nodded maniacally. “Yeah. I did. We had a deal. And I couldn’t fulfill my half. He out-bargained a goblin.” I muttered a curse and kicked a random car tire.

Scharf glanced at the prison. “Not here. Get in the car.”

“What?”

“Let’s go,” she repeated and jogged towards her sedan.

I glanced back at the building once and followed. She didn’t turn the car on but just said, “He’s a sociopath. He’s incapable of feeling anything.”

I twisted to poke her in the arm. “Exactly! I mean, he can fake it, but he’s not genuinely happy. So, I didn’t hold up my end of the bargain. And now I fascinated a serial killer!”

“What if he isn’t a serial killer?” Scharf said, fastening her seat belt. “What if he’s telling the truth?”

I shook my head. “I don’t know anything anymore. He might be innocent, and we have locked up the wrong man. Or Fuller did it to return to his former glory. Or someone else copied Hastings—someone we haven’t even met yet.”

She started the car, then slammed the steering wheel with her palm. “Hell, I could have done it.”

That alarmed me for half a second before I remembered I was her alibi.

I blew out a breath as Scharf continued, “And accusing yourself of murder is not a valid investigative technique.”

“Right? So what should we do?” I fought my panic. What do we do?

She pulled out of the parking lot at a speed that elevated my heart rate a little as she maneuvered toward the interstate.

I fiddled with the radio, but there was no music anywhere. NPR came through loud and clear as someone explained why radio waves for music were not working near DC. They said it was something to do with weather, bass, and cell phones. I rolled my eyes. They announced the next program about the DC crime wave, and I clicked it off.

“Even if he didn’t kill anyone,” I said, “I don’t think he’s in the wrong place.”

“I didn’t hear that.”

“It’s not up to me anyway, so it doesn’t matter what I think.”

She glanced at me. “Sometimes it’s up to you.”

“What are you talking about?”

Scharf sped up carefully in the icy, wet weather as we got on the highway, heading south. In my secret heart of hearts, I was enjoying the ice on the roads with Scharf behind the wheel. Normally, she drove like she was on a NASCAR racetrack. But with such treacherous conditions, she drove like a normal insane DC driver—a vast improvement.

“There are some creatures who don’t break the law because there aren’t any laws for what they do. And then you step in.”

“Oh, no. I don’t want to play judge, jury, or executioner. Especially not that last one.”

She shrugged. “You’ve already done it.”

“When?”

“With the djinni.”

“He deserved what he got.”

She raised an eyebrow, and I sighed. “Touché.”

Scharf gripped the steering wheel hard. “Whoever took out that goblin behind the teashop isn’t human, right? How will we lock up the killer if he possesses that kind of strength?” Scharf hooked a thumb behind us. “That was a Supermax. The strictest protocols we have. Could you have gotten out of there? You’ve already escaped one prison.”

“As you pointed out to me at the time, I wasn’t in prison. I was in jail in Metro Police custody. But to answer your question, sure. It would have taken me fifteen minutes to get out of there. I would’ve followed you out.”

She smiled and maneuvered around a trundling semi.

I couldn’t let this go. “I don’t know how to punish rogue goblin killers. How do I make sure it’s fair? Why is this my job?”

“Aren’t you the leader of goblins? Don’t you guys have a court of law or something?”

“Fairies have courts if the stories are right. Two of them. If goblins ever got organized enough to hold trials, it would involve tons of cleaning and a severe amount of sugar.” I flashed her a smile. “Not any kind of judgment. And I don’t think ‘leader’ is the right title.”

“Peallaidh, or whatever your title is, you’re making the calls, right?”

I slumped in my seat. “Nobody will tell me! It would unfairly balance the challenge, I guess. It sounds like all that the goblins need now in a leader is a punching bag to test themselves against. Until I can prove I’m better than all of them, they’re leaving me out of things. Or is it stronger? I still don’t know the criteria. For all I know, my challenger and I could both enter the Olympic decathlon and throw javelins and shoot people, and then we’d know.”

Scharf laughed. “I don’t think there’s shooting in the decathlon.”

“Then what’s the shooting one?”

She thought for a second. “That would be the biathlon: shooting and skiing. The decathlon is running, high jump, shot put… I forget the rest. Summer Olympics.”

“I can’t even keep the winter and summer Olympics straight! What the hell’s wrong with me?” I told myself that my foul mood was artificial, but it didn’t feel like that.

“You’re doing great, Orange. Hastings is a bad guy. It took the FBI years to track him down and put him away.”

“You’re thanking me for failing. Thank you for thanking me for failing.”

She turned toward the beltway around DC, and we fell silent as she merged onto the ten-lane road. When we were thoroughly stuck in a mile of backed-up vehicles, I realized why Jack would want to commit homicide after any length of time in this. Workers had obviously de-iced the huge road that encircled the city, but it was already building up again. I’d read something online about how many millions it had cost the city to keep the ice at bay over the past few days.

When she missed the exit for downtown, I frowned. “Aren’t we investigating things?”

She shook her head. “Oh, no, we have something much more important to do.”

“More important than finding the killer, or both killers, and the ice storm instigator? All of whom might be the same or different people.”

“We’re going to do something fun.”

“Who are you and what have you done with Scharf?”

“That was our turn back there,” Scharf answered. I looked behind us at the retreating exit. “I couldn’t make myself take it.”

“What?”

“I can’t make myself go back into the city.” She sounded more bemused by this than anything else. Scharf was as mundane as it got. I assumed she meant she didn’t want to, not that there was an invisible force keeping her from turning a steering wheel. Just normal human dread, Orange.

“And now you’re going to say I have no idea what fun is, right?” Her fingers were white on the steering wheel.

“How about we don’t make any assumptions about what the other person is thinking for the next few days? If you have an irrational thought about how the world is attacking you, blame it on the weather.” I winced, remembering the sickly crunch of the goblin’s nose beneath my fists.

“Your honor, the rain made me do it!” Though she sounded grumpy, the heat had left her voice.

“You’re not inside my head; I’m not inside your head, so when you make up a reason for what I’m saying, make up a good one. Where do you want to go instead?” I chewed my bottom lip. “What do people do for fun around here?”

“Look at spaceships or statues of old dead men?”

“How about outside of the city?” I thought for a second. “Are there any waterfalls around?”

“Waterfalls are fun?”

I cleared my throat. “Apparently, my father enjoyed them.” I’d spent the drive to the prison distracting myself with more information about my father. Or about an ancient goblin from Scotland with the same name as my father. There wasn’t exactly an Ancestry.com entry for immortals. “Or he created waterfalls? Maybe.”

Scharf turned and blinked at me.

“I’ll make a deal with you,” I said. “I won’t attempt any kind of joke while we’re in a moving vehicle if you keep your eyes glued to the road at all times.”

She snorted and turned toward the highway again. “What about the odometer? I can’t look at that? You don’t care how fast I drive?”

“Okay. And the rearview mirror and the side-view mirrors and your blind spot. Never mind—that’s a terrible bargain. There are too many places to look. I take it back. Keep your eyes on the road. And all the other above’s.”

Scharf relaxed and exited the beltway onto a winding street through bare trees. After a mile or so, she said, “That sounded like it was news to you—about the waterfalls?”

I dug out my phone. “Yeah, and it turns out he was potentially a bit older than I thought.”

“What, like that artist who was in his eighties when he had a kid?”

“He wasn’t Mick Jagger!”

“Um, not the artist I was referring to. How old, Orange?”

I closed my eyes. “A couple of millennia, maybe?” As the car swerved, my eyes flew open. “Eyes on the road!”

“I’m fine!” She gripped the steering wheel tighter, but she was panting. It was the strongest reaction I’d seen from her. “Millennia. That’s not an exaggeration. The plural of millennium.”

“Yep.” When she stayed more or less straight for another few blocks, I said, “I did a bit more research about my kind.”

“Googling yourself? You’ve never done it before?”

“Of course, I have. I studied folklore and goblins for years. I’d see Peallaidh and figure it was a common Scottish name. Like if your last name is… I don’t know… Churchill and then you read a biography of Winston Churchill, and you know you’re not related to him, but it’s kind of cool.”

“So, did you learn anything more about your father?”

I nodded. “The Peallaidh was associated with the village of Aberfeldy in Scotland. He was a leader of a type of goblin called urusk, who liked the outdoors as much as home and hearth. Urusks are hobgoblins, so they’re a little bigger and more powerful than regular goblins. And they enjoy the water.”

“The box?”

“There was a waterfall in Scotland my father liked. There’s not a lot of information, even online. But there used to be gatherings in Scotland of goblins where… Well, I’m not sure what they would do, probably play a bunch of jokes on each other and make some good food.”

“Sounds like fun.”

“Fun is important.”

She grinned. “We have a waterfall near DC, the Great Falls in Virginia. About half an hour from here. They’re kind of famous, I think. Or they’re close, so that’s where the tourists go. Jen and I went there when we first moved here.”

I thought of what to say but left the subject of her ex-girlfriend alone, not wanting to rile her up again. “I suppose there isn’t a lot of water in Arizona.”

“God, no. Though it’s balmy compared to Afghanistan in the summer.”

“Why am I asking you about fun things to do around here? You have the least fun of anybody I’ve ever met.” As soon as I said it, I regretted it.

“You know, Orange…” she trailed off, sounding a little hurt.

“Never mind. That was an exaggeration. You have a lot of fun,” I said, although that was an exaggeration too. As she closed her eyes for a moment, I said, “The road!”

“Fun isn’t always about jokes and playing around,” she said, her eyes snapping open. “You can throw yourself into something meaningful and have it… I don’t know.”

“Mean something?”

“Exactly.”

I wanted to cross my arms and stop speaking to her, which told me whoever was affecting people was still working his terrible magic. Instead, I tried to keep this bizarre conversation going. “I like to bake. Don’t snicker. It’s more of a commitment than a knock-knock joke, so I get more out of it. Can we go see the waterfall?”

She glanced at me. “Sure.”

“Knock knock.”

“Oh, God.”

“Interrupting—”

“Don’t even try it. Hassan warned me.”

I slumped in my seat.

We slowly wound our way out of DC, and with every mile we put between us and DC, the vice at the back of my neck released a little, and my shoulders stretched away from my ears. Although, as the weather improved, Scharf sped up considerably.

“It’s not everywhere,” I said when we were firmly enfolded in bare trees. There were traces of ice on the ground, but many more fallen leaves and mulch.

She glanced at me.

I hit the button for the radio, and for the first time in days, heard new music. Something canned and auto-tuned, but it sounded like a symphony. I pointed out the window. “The ice, the feeling like you’re going to explode. The music.”

She glanced out at the trees. “The further we get from DC, the better it feels. I hadn’t even realized how stressed I was.” She stretched an arm above her head.

“If it’s localized, we can find it,” I said.

“How? The localized part is the entire city.”

“I don’t know.”

She glanced at me, and I had to bite my tongue to keep from telling her to look at the road.

“What magical thing can affect the weather of half the Eastern seaboard?”

I winced; that was exactly the kind of question I was here to answer, but I had no clue. “I’m—still researching. There are both too many things and far too few.”

“Explain.”

“Every folklore tradition on earth has beings that can affect the weather. But something this powerful? I don’t know. And you can’t get around the dead goblin. We’re tough to kill. I’m not being arrogant when I say that.”

“No,” she said, “I’ve seen you not die many times when you should have.”

“Funny.” I sighed, trying but failing to find a comeback. “Insert clever pun here.”

Her mouth dropped open. “You can’t think of a joke? Orange Sinclair? Something is wrong.”

“A priest, a rabbi—”

“Absolutely not.”

She turned onto a smaller, curvier road through the leafless, black trees.

“It’s not only the ice,” she continued. “We’re in the middle of an unprecedented storm that should keep everyone hunkered down, yet we’ve also got an unprecedented crime wave.”

“What crime wave?”

“You remember Detective Lee?”

I laughed. “He’s memorable.” He’d arrested me and was the first person I disappeared on.

“Normally, when the weather gets bad, crime goes down, but they’re getting run off their feet with thefts and assaults. The Bureau is sending agents to deal with petty crime. We’ve got guys from the Hoover Building who’ve been out of the field for a decade responding to drunk and disorderlies. It seems everybody’s fuse got snipped in half, and we’ve burned through what’s left.” She rolled her neck, and it cracked three times. “Don’t think about it now. I’m just starting to feel competent again.”

I suppressed a smile. Of course, for Scharf, the most terrible symptom would be incompetence. Not actual incompetence, only feeling like it. The smile died. For me, the most terrible symptom was punching goblins in the face. She was right. Put it down, Orange. For an hour.

Great Falls National Park was in the wooded foothills of McLean, Virginia. Scharf flashed her badge to get us in at the ranger station. By the time we hit the trail to the falls, the feeling of normalcy and even optimism felt a little euphoric. When the trail petered out thirty feet from the guest house, my enthusiasm quickly fizzled.

“This is it?”

She spread out her hands. “This is it.”

A wide river spread in front of us, either the Anacostia or the Potomac; I still hadn’t figured out which was which. The water tumbled over a short stack of rocks. It was maybe a twenty-foot drop if I was being very generous.

And most of it was ice. I’d first visited Yosemite when I was six, with its waterfalls the size of skyscrapers. The sense of disappointment was familiar as I looked out at the three families snapping pictures, all looking very satisfied.

I turned to Scharf. “Well, you’re right, no fun to be had today.”

I couldn’t even wade out into the water. Giant rocks and rails stood in the way and the hypothermia definitely wouldn’t be fun.

Scharf pulled me toward the spray. “Come on, try something.”

I looked at her like she was crazy.

“You can pull down buildings with your earth power. You’re telling me the box water had nothing to do with magic? If we’re fighting something that controls the weather, I don’t know how much concrete will help. You could use this.”

I turned to the pathetic falls and climbed up a flat boulder to get a better look. Up here, I was in the freezing spray from the falls. It felt like little ice kisses.

I didn’t have much faith that anything was going to change. I also hoped I would not gain any more powers; the last thing I wanted was to one day be able to manipulate the air and then suffocate in my sleep from my incompetence.

Don’t borrow trouble, I told myself.

I closed my eyes and reached out. Magic felt like a sixth sense; somehow, I plugged into the world and sensed earth as if it were a part of me. Immediately my body felt heavy, and the boulders came into my awareness. They bore up underneath the incredible pressure from the water. They felt harassed, though, because the water was slowly winning.

As I tried to reach for the water and move it, I felt a dim, much more fluid presence, though I was half sure I imagined it. I was standing before a mighty river; it should have been louder than the rocks in my awareness.

A gentle rain sprinkled down on us, but I couldn’t move it either. Not even a drop flickered.

I turned and looked at Scharf. “We could go bowling.”

Grinning, she stood with her arms crossed and her feet far apart. “I’m enjoying this a heck of a lot, actually.”

I gritted my teeth, turned, and tried again. When my earth magic manifested, it had been out of sheer necessity. I’d been running for my life from a fire-breathing nightmare, and I had literally run through a wall.

“Push me off or something.”

Scharf’s jaw slackened. “No.”

“Then do something else threatening!”

She shook her head. I looked out at the spray. No change.

A gasp came from below me. Scharf pointed above my head. I looked up and said, “What?”

“Now that’s a superb skill to have.”

“What?” I repeated.

“Look up!”

“I am!” And then I saw it. The rain parted around me as if I were carrying an invisible umbrella.

Laughing, I raised my hand to stick my fingers in the cold spray. When I tried to widen the umbrella, it quickly disappeared.

I did have water magic, but it had a radius of about one foot. I extended my hand, this time pushing at the water, and managed to push it farther, but as soon as I dropped my hand, the bubble returned to a foot away from my head. “If you ever need to levitate some water a few inches, I’m your girl.”

“You can’t move the falls at all?”

A giggle escaped. “Not a bit. Seriously.”

I collected a bit of water in my hands. “If this is the great power of the Peallaidh, I’m the world’s wimpiest water goblin.” Another cackle burst out of my throat.

Scharf laughed too, then sobered up. “I shouldn’t be laughing. This really isn’t funny.”

I climbed off the boulder, quite relieved to be staying dry and still laughing. “What on earth is this gonna do for me? How can I pull down buildings with the earth but be so wimpy with water?”

“You’re asking me?”

“Well, I can’t ask Google.” I sighed. “Oh, God, I’m going to have to go talk to Mrs. Henderson again.”

“What would she know?”

“Never mind!”

Scharf guffawed. “Why are we laughing?”

“I have no idea. Maybe because we’re totally screwed!”

“That’s it!”

She fisted her hands on her knees so she could get some extra air.

I snorted again. “We’ve got some unknown magical creature who can freeze the entire city, and I can almost keep the rain off my hair. This is going so well!”

Scharf stood up. “We’ll pull it out of the hat, Orange.”

“What hat?”

“We’ll find a way. We always have.”

I felt even more hysterical. “By ‘always,’ you mean once before. We defeated one djinni, who wanted us to defeat him, by the way.”

Scharf burst out laughing again. “Oh, yeah! He wanted our help! That’s how we defeated him!”

“We’re so dead!”

She let a high-pitched giggle out that I’d never heard before. “We’re so dead!”

I wiped tears from my eyes, then flicked them from the edge of my fingers and sent them spiraling into the rain. Sure enough, two feet from my body, I lost track of them.

Scharf goggled. “That was impressive!”

“I know, right?”

Scharf hiccupped.

“I feel drunk!” I said.

“No, I feel high. Drunk is sad,” Scharf said, glancing at her weapon hand like it didn’t belong to her.

“Scharf! You got high!” I said with a giggle.

“Didn’t!”

“You just said!”

“I said I don’t like alcohol,” she said primly, but I knew what I heard. “It was only for law enforcement purposes.”

I rolled my eyes. “There’s no law enforcement purpose to a trip. It’s okay. You can be human once in a while. I won’t tell.”

Scharf froze, all humor leaching from her face.

“What?”

“Inhuman.”

I jerked, stung.

“Not you!” she said and paced away. Her energy was so different away from the storm. “What he said.”

“I remember,” I said, fighting the urge to cross my arms in defense against the hate in his eyes.

“Not that part. The part about drunks.”

I scratched my head, replaying the interview. He’d said something about drunks, but what stuck in my mind is that last look and laugh.

Scharf turned to me. “You said to him that the victims didn’t stick a fork in their own eyes.”

I forced my brain back beyond that final look. “And he said they could have because they were drunks and inhuman.”

She scrubbed at her forehead and had to pace again. “We missed something huge.”

“Hey, you don’t have to rub it in my face!” I said. “I’ll remember the next time we interview a serial killer not to ask them to emote.”

“No, not you, the FBI. Fuller missed something huge in the original investigation! I need the autopsies.”

“Why?” I asked and had to dash after her when she started towards the car. “What are we looking for? What did he miss?”

She stopped. “Hastings sure hated drunk people.”

“Inhuman,” I echoed, shuddering.

“Maybe he hated them enough to eliminate them. Maybe enough that it was a part of his motive. His victims had to be intoxicated.”

At last, I got it. “And if all the victims were three sheets to the wind, but Rodger wasn’t…”

“If they weren’t what?” Scharf asked.

I blinked. “Three sheets to the wind. Drunk? You’ve never heard that expression?”

“Never. If they were, um, three sheets to the wind, this is a copycat.”

I had been with her until that last statement. “Why?”

“It’s Rodger himself. Conscientious kid at work on a super busy day, taking powerful epilepsy meds you can’t mix with alcohol. The killer could fake everything else about the scene, but not his blood alcohol level.”

It was at moments like these that I realized investigating is as much art as science. We couldn’t absolutely know why Hastings had done what he’d done, or that Rodger wouldn’t drink, but it made a lot of sense. “If Hastings only killed drunk people, Rodger was a copycat.”

“Which means John Hastings should be in jail. I need those autopsies.” She started walking again at twice her normal pace, which was saying something.

I jogged after her, fighting to believe her. We’d have our answer if she was right about Hastings and right about Rodger and right about the motive and right about…

I took a last look at the waterfall behind me. Out of Scharf’s sight, a few tears leaked down my cheeks. I let my umbrella go so they could mix with the rain. We’d gone toe to toe with a serial killer and released the Peallaidh’s special water powers, and that was it. I could make an umbrella and he maybe only killed drunk people and Rodger maybe wasn’t drunk.

There was no trace of my laughter in my voice as I whispered, “We are so dead.”

14

As Scharf drove at her ice-reduced pace into the city, and barren black trees gave way to cement buildings, I fell asleep. When I woke up in downtown DC, I tried not to read it as a terrible sign that I could sleep through Scharf driving in an ice storm. That pointed to a level of exhaustion I hadn’t experienced before.

As I watched the red and silver taxis, Metro buses, and fancy black SUVs drive by, I sighed. Most people were taking the subway if they could stay out of the ice. Nobody had the tires for this. “Even if you can prove Hastings didn’t do it, we still have to prove who did.”

Her fingers whitened on the steering wheel. “You called it.”

“I did?”

“Law enforcement. Not just any copycat, a copycat that could mimic the exact details of the crime scene.”

She maneuvered around a Circle line bus, the cleaner, cheaper buses DC created for tourists that only went around downtown because the people living and working in the suburbs didn’t need cheaper, nicer transportation.

I really was grumpy. “Am I going to interrogate one of the premier interrogators in the FBI? Because I don’t think that’s going to go well.”

Scharf shook her head. “I’d love to get the truth out of him, but one of your bargains is not proof. We need evidence he did it or an affirmative confession, not just a denial in front of you.”

I watched a big SUV with embassy plates play chicken on the ice with the other cars at an intersection. Fortunately, before we had to try to get through ourselves, Scharf turned into a parking garage halfway down the block. I hadn’t realized we’d arrived.

Her office was in one of the ubiquitous buildings downtown with a clothing shop on the bottom floor and a variety of lobbyists, lawyers, and other esoteric private and government businesses on the floors above, including one of the DC FBI field offices. It was a small set of cubicles and conference rooms on the fourth floor.

FBI Headquarters were right on the National Mall in the Hoover Building, but she was assigned to the local violent crimes office, a field office like hundreds around the country that happened to be next door to the national headquarters.

We made our way to the fourth floor through the beige hallways.

“You should probably…”

“Invisible. Oh, I know.”

I told the world not to notice me but did not tell Scharf that Mrs. Henderson would see me regardless. Scharf swiped her wallet at the keycard machine at the end of the hall and led us into chaos.

Somebody shouted into a phone; I dodged out of the way of one running agent, and some bigwig in a suit was bellowing in his office beyond felt-walled cubicles, all buried in towers of paper and aging computers. I headed to the right to another glass door that said, “Violet Crimes.” The N had long since fallen off the door.

Now people were calling her office “purple” on purpose. Scharf’s unit was gaining a reputation as the place to send inexplicable crimes: bodies that didn’t quite look human or people who’d been killed for no obvious reason.

It was probably a good thing their admin was a goblin.

To most people, she looked like a little person with brown skin, long black hair, and a serious fondness for gold. To my eyes, she looked like an olive-green goblin with long, pointed, and ragged ears, and sharp teeth.

Mrs. Henderson had known my father and helped a lot with the previous case Scharf and I had worked on. I’d never seen tip nor tail of Mr. Henderson, but she insisted on the honorific. Scharf gave her a nod and moved to her felt-walled cubicle behind her.

Scharf logged onto her wheezing computer that still had an off-white monitor that took up half her desk. I didn’t follow.

When I cleared my throat, Mrs. Henderson looked up and groaned. I was looking forward to seeing her again but stopped short when a terrible thought occurred. She was a goblin. “D-do you want to challenge me?”

Her groan turned into a smile. She said in a scratchy, low voice, “So it started?”

“You don’t want to challenge me?”

Scharf stood up, and I got ready to go, but she went to the printer wedged into the back corner of her row of cubicles, waiting for sheets to spit out at a glacial crank.

Mrs. Henderson shook her head. “I won’t be in line for weeks yet.”

“Scharf? With me.” We all looked up at the linebacker-sized dude standing in the door of an office crooking a finger at Scharf.

“Sir?” She straightened, but he’d already retreated into the office. She held up a finger to me, bolted into the office, and closed the door.

I looked at Mrs. Henderson. “But you’re going to challenge me?”

“They start with the small fries.”

I clenched my fists. “The last one didn’t feel very small.”

She looked me up and down with a grin. “But you defeated him, correct? What did he do—jump off a bridge, drown, bury himself alive?”

I heaved a sigh. “What am I? A gladiator at the Colosseum, fighting for your amusement? It wasn’t funny.”

Mrs. Henderson smirked, her sharp teeth gleaming in the fluorescent light. “It’s a little bit funny.”

Someone peeked over a nearby cubicle wall. “You okay?”

She tsked and said, “Phone call.” She picked up and put down her receiver, then closed her eyes and muttered something. I felt the air shift as she opened her eyes. “And now I’m invisible to my own coworkers. Thank you very much.”

“How is that my fault?”

“What do you want?” She meant: go away.
