I stood with a wooden stake in one hand and a hammer in the other, surveying Scharf’s crawlspace. It smelled of wet clay and ever so faintly of char. A month ago, I’d defeated a load of ghuls by smothering them with the earth down here. I brushed my feet across the ground and uncovered the new heap of obsidian rock I’d created.

At the edge of the glassy black hump, I hammered the stake into the ground.

I heard a fart and turned around. The garden gnome Grimm was standing on his head, staring at me. “Can’t grow tomatoes here, you know.”

“I did know that Grimm. That’s not what this is for.”

I picked up the square bit of plywood and nailed it to the stake.

I stood back so he could see.

“What do the marks say?” Of course, he wouldn’t know. When would a garden gnome learn to read? His brain had to be the size of a peanut.

“Try me,” I said, crossing my arms.

“I don't want to fight!”

“No, that’s what the marks say: ‘Try me.’”

“How do you want them to try you?”

I grabbed my father’s box. I had stayed up for eight hours straight, trying to get more buttons to depress. Eight stairs on each side had moved, and the lid had opened the same centimeter, but no more water had come out, and nothing else had happened.

I sighed and placed it at the base of the stake.

“I defeated the Lubber Fiend, and if there’s a goblin more powerful than him or anyone else interested in challenging me, they can try me.” I was the Peallaidh. Now I had to figure out what that meant.

“Try me!” I spoke to the empty crawlspace and the gnome. I looked down at him and said, “That means you, too.”

“Little gnomes don’t challenge the Peallaidh.” He rolled closer to the box and bowed to it. Then he stood up and saluted me with his police hat. He paused and waited.

I vaguely remembered movies where people had to salute back, so I smashed my hand to my forehead. He nodded, and with another fart, started rooting in the dirt.

“How would you like to be appointed guardian of the Peallaidh’s box?” I asked him.

He looked at me with glowing plastic blue eyes. “Oh, yes.”

As he stood stiffly at attention, I searched around for something official to give him, but the crawlspace was empty except for Scharf’s canoe equipment, which I'd banished from the living room. I grabbed an oar and bopped him carefully on both shoulders, aping a ceremony I’d read in King Arthur and His Knights of the Round Table. “I appoint thee guardian of the box.”

He took up his post with solemnity.

“You don’t have to stay down here,” I said.

“But I am the guardian.”

I felt bad about what I’d started but better about my chances of keeping the box safe. I brushed off my hands on my jeans and turned to the crawlspace entrance: a trapdoor into the garden. No stairs led into the house directly.

Which was how I ended up tramping through three inches of mud at the side of the house. The ice storm had broken when the dragon laughed. Now, days later, a few spikes of green stuck out of the ground, although that was undoubtedly the gnome’s doing; most of the houses on this street were still stuck in deep, slushy, muddy winter.

As I picked my way through the puddles, I dug out my cell phone and dialed Jack, my heart thumping.

“Jack.”

“Hey, it’s Orange.”

“Oh, so now you’re calling? You know, most people who forced their boyfriend to leave town would send them a message that they never wanted to see them again.”

“Boyfriend?”

“Can we have the talk and start saying ‘we’ like normal people who date?”

I rolled my eyes and propped a hand on my hip. “The Peallaidh and a random demon with the power to flatten the city between us? Absolutely, let’s behave like normal, well-adjusted people.”

“At least one of us is.”

“If you don’t want me to hang up,” I said, “don’t tell me which one of us you’re referring to.”

“Grilled cheese sounds lovely.”

“You know, when I saw you at Union Station, I thought you were the coolest person alive, but I didn’t realize you have no game whatsoever.”

Jack scoffed. “There are so many more important things than game, Orange. If you would get over here, you could enjoy them.”

“And arrogant to beat.”

“And you love it. Tonight? Seven?”

“Fine,” I said, feigning annoyance.

I could hear his smile over the phone. “I’ll pick you up?”

“Sure,” I said, my mouth crinkling.

“We’ve succeeded at the whole dating thing. And we arranged a dinner without lighting anything on fire or killing a magical creature or ourselves.”

“That’s huge for us.”

“I’ll see you soon.”

It surprised me to feel my cheeks heating. I’d missed him. “You okay? Did you really go to New York?”

“Well, at first, I thought you were being paranoid and trying to break up with me in a bizarre, Orange way, but then I got a migraine and contemplated how great it would be to barbecue the restaurant’s hostess, who had overbooked one table. As soon as I realized I wanted to extinguish a human being, I got on a train. My migraine disappeared at the Maryland border. So I guess I have you to thank for that as well.”

I took a deep breath. “Thank you for leaving town, Jack.”

“Thank you for warning me. It’s almost like you care.”

“Oh, no,” I said, “We had the label discussion, but we aren’t having the caring discussion right now. I can’t handle two discussions in one phone call.”

“Fair enough, but now you know it’s coming.” Why did that statement fill me with such dread? And was I really surprised to realize that I was the panicked one in this relationship? Jack had jumped in with both feet the moment he saw me; it should flatter me. But no, it worried me. I tried to put that aside yet again.

“Fine,” I said. “I may or may not care about you, but I’ll see you tonight.”

“That’s as good as I’m going to get, isn’t it?”

“You’d better believe it.”

Throughout the last half of our conversation, I was standing in a mud puddle, slowly sinking. I hung up, pulled my feet out, and sloshed my way to the front of the house.

And then I froze and mentally shouted, “Don’t notice me!”

Scharf stood on her front porch, facing a stranger with her hand on her belt where her gun normally rested. It was one of her few tells when she was nervous. Her feet were planted, and her jawline was white.

I wanted to get closer but realized I’d track muddy prints up to her porch, so I snuck along the empty flower bed to get close enough to hear the guy she was confronting.

On the surface, nothing was threatening about him. But he was some kind of Fed. I was beginning to recognize the look. The ill-fitting suit, red tie, buzz cut. He packed a few extra pounds over the requisite muscles. His face was ruddy and carved with laugh lines, which I didn’t expect. But he wasn’t laughing now.

“What do you want from me?” Scharf asked.

“I want an introduction. You can reach their world.”

“What world?”

“We call it the unexplained. We’re collecting more and more evidence that the simple laws of physics aren’t sufficient, nor are those of law enforcement.”

Scharf’s stance widened a touch more. “Then you’d better talk to Congress about making some new laws.”

“Don’t play coy. You got on my radar during the incident on inauguration day. But anyone can get lucky once. Now you’ve solved the most talked-about homicide in the city, and I don’t believe it’s pure luck.”

Sweat trickled down my spine, and my heart pounded. He was talking about magic.

“It wasn’t a homicide.” Scharf crossed her arms. “And maybe I’m just good at my job.”

She leaned forward on the balls of her toes, preparing for action.

“One of them trusts you,” the Fed said. “I don’t want to mess with that—far from it. I want its help.”

It took me a second to realize by “it,” he meant me.

Scharf’s eyes unerringly met mine over his shoulder, and she asked, “What do you have in mind?”

CELTIC SHIELD CHAPTER 1

Buy Now

“Here we go again,” I whispered as I approached the crime scene tape.

The museum exhibit was supposed to have a plethora of beautiful art, but now all empty displays were piled with intricate swirls of dirt and surrounded by law enforcement officers with an alphabet soup of abbreviations on their backs.

I whispered, “Scharf!”

A black head of short hair popped up, and Special Agent Laura Scharf headed my way. She wore a blue nylon blazer stamped in yellow letters spelling out FBI over a white button-down shirt and black suit pants.

She smiled for .2 seconds. “Glad you could make it.”

“Where’s the fairy?” This was the bargain we’d made: I help her with supernatural crimes in exchange for not arresting me. Whatever sadistic scorekeeper in my head that kept track of these bargains had decided the scales were not balanced yet. But the complete lack of fairies, djinnis, dragons, or goblins in the room meant this was outside of my jurisdiction.

Scharf shooed me back out of the exhibit and dangled pairs of gloves and booties in my face. I sighed and put them on. I’d lost the thou-must-wear-gloves argument at our first crime scene together a few months ago.

“Thanks for coming,” she repeated.

“There is absolutely nothing magical about stealing a bunch of dirt.” I pointed a gloved hand at the general melee.

“Lee is getting a nose for these things. When he said it was something special for me, I believe him.” She gestured to a familiar man in a ratty blue blazer looking at an empty pedestal in the glare of a police light. Though he had no letters on his back, I knew he was Metro PD because he was the first person to arrest me when I got to DC. It had been a rough year.

Technically, he arrested me on New Year’s Eve, so this year was going better? In three months I’d destroyed Union Station, got arrested, and almost got killed three times by a djinni, a goblin, and a dragon. This year was not going better.

I crossed my arms. “Lee thinks I’m your quirky roommate who should be in jail.”

She raised an eyebrow. “My expert consultant who occasionally, with no one the wiser, sleeps on my couch. And a Presidential pardon, so no more getting arrested.” She stopped before reentering the archway back into the empty exhibit. “Unless you’ve done something else he should arrest you for?”

I shook my head vigorously. I cast my mind over the last few weeks; it was even true. Since I’d defeated the challenger for my father’s throne, it had been a quiet month.

My father had been the king of goblins. From him, I’d inherited a box I still couldn’t open, a multitude of hobgoblin powers I was still figuring out, and his kingdom. Though that didn’t seem to be as big of a commitment as I thought, which shouldn’t have surprised me. Goblins don’t really go for group activities.

Scharf waved at Detective Lee who was now speaking to somebody in a full-body white jumpsuit.

Scharf took a slow deep breath and said in her FBI voice, “Orange.”

“Seriously, what am I doing here? I can’t help gather evidence! Or magically divine… Whatever was in here.”

“Irish arts and crafts.”

I felt a whisper of disquiet when I heard Irish. I tugged at my very orange curls. My father was from Scotland, as were many magical creatures. I don’t know why we collected on tiny islands in the North Atlantic. I personally suspected it was the mist. You can get away with a lot of magic when it’s foggy.

Washington DC is not very foggy. Grey, cold, with this spitting sleet that TV weather personalities called “wintry mix,” but not very foggy. So if the Irish were involved, there was an outside chance this was magical.

Except… “This is the American History Museum. Why is there a display of Irish arts and crafts?”

“Irish-American arts and crafts,” Scharf said, “Made by immigrants or descendants. That part’s not clear.”

My shiver died. “Sticking an infinity knot on a jug does not make this Celtic art.”

There was nothing Irish about Scharf. She was German pale with black hair and stubborn cheekbones. Actually, every one of her bones was stubborn. Her stick-up-the-ass posture came from her time in the army though, not her heritage.

She took another step into the light, “We have work to do.”

“Sure, when there’s another murdering creature on the loose, I’m your goblin, but this is no business of the Nnn….whatever they’re calling it.”

“The National Taskforce on the Efficacy of Partnering with the Unexplained for Enforcing the Law,” Scharf said quickly.

“I can’t imagine why I didn’t remember that. Are you going to trade your blazer for one that says NTEPUEL?”

Yes, the word was out, at least among the nation’s top-secret agencies that there is a good chance not everyone on earth is precisely human. And our last case was just a little too dramatic to hide the fact that Scharf was closing cases with supernatural help. And what did our nation’s most secret government servants do about this world-view-shattering fact?

If you guessed form a task force, you get a pony. In a moment of surviving-a-dragon-induced optimism, I agreed to join it. Well, that and Scharf sleeps with a gun. Not that she’d ever point it at me—again. I just wanted to mention that fact in relation to this decision.

It’s just that my expertise came from eleven semesters of folklore classes from the anthropology degree I’d abandoned, and three months as the heir of the goblin king. If that sounds like insufficient preparation for protecting the country and defending the constitution or whatever it was Scharf is always going on about, then you’re right.

“Lee can’t even see me!”

She said through gritted teeth, “You can’t stay invisible forever.”

She wasn’t speaking metaphorically. I always had the ability to disappear and know the escape route out of every situation. I was 100% okay with goblins being the cowards of the supernatural community.

As I thought of escaping, an orange line only I could see led out of the exhibit and up the stairs to the eight glass doors at the front of the museum, presenting me with the fastest escape route.

Yes, thank you, I said to my magic with a roll of my eyes. The line disappeared.

“Scharf!” Detective Lee said as he hitched up his pants and came over. “You came alone? This almost makes me miss Orange.” He glanced around quickly. “You didn’t bring her, did you?”

From my angle, I could see her right-hand flex, one of her only tells. Sometimes she couldn’t control the enthusiasm of her weapon hand. “I can solve crimes without consultants.”

I played it back in my head. It was technically not a lie. I didn’t know whether to feel proud or upset that she was getting sneakier. Scharf was the most honest person I knew; she never lied until she met me.

Lee winced. “I don’t know if any of us can solve this one without somebody’s help.”

I sighed and retreated out of his direct view and let go of the focus that let me go unnoticed. That was technically what happened. I didn’t disappear; it’s just that no one noticed me.

Well, no one except Scharf. I hadn’t successfully disappeared in front of her in weeks. Scharf says that it’s just that she never takes her eyes off a threat. She finds that uproariously funny every time she says it. I laugh too because, you know, she’s armed.

I stepped closer and waved at Lee, who did not have Scharf’s poker face. He squeaked and jumped. I didn’t blame him. I disappeared out of his locked police car and handcuffs the first time I figured out this unnoticing thing. He didn’t take it well.

“Orange!” He said with false heartiness. “Didn’t see you there.”

I bit my lip. “How strange. I’ve been standing here the whole time.”

I am not as good a person as Scharf.

“Why are we here?” Scharf asked.

Lee scratched at his chin and pointed. “Come look at this.”

We walked over to the central exhibit, a low wide pedestal in white, now covered in dirt. I read the plaque next to it. It had a picture of the artifact that had been on the dais. A beautiful, mahogany table and chair set with elaborately carved backs in a definite Celtic style. “Appalachian table and four chairs. Circa 1900s.”

“Look,” Lee insisted.

With Scharf, I leaned over the dais to stare at the dirt to see a five-pointed star traced with elaborate knots and swirls. I laughed; I couldn’t help it.

Scharf, Lee, and a couple of techs looked at me.

“Sorry.” I pointed to the picture. “The backs of the chair had the same design: a star amongst leaves. And knots.”

“So this is not a satanic ritual?” Lee asked. “A couple years ago a kid carved pentagrams into his friend in a satanic ritual. I mean, it wasn't a satanic ritual, he was just awful, but he said that’s what he was trying to do. I was still on the beat.”

I shook my head. This is one subject we had covered in my folklore classes, probably to forestall this exact conversation when students found pentagrams everywhere. “I mean, I’m just guessing here, but pentagrams are found in almost every culture. Most of the time, they have pretty positive connotations.”

Scharf crossed her arms. “Like what?”

“In Celtic mythology? No one knows for sure because the druids didn’t write anything down. They think it’s a symbol of the feminine. All stages of womanhood - maiden, mother, crone, light, and dark. That’s just the Celts. You would get a totally different answer in India, China, the Middle East, and Africa.”

“That’s a lot of different options,” Scharf said at last, and she looked around at other displays.

“They all have designs on them,” Lee said. “But this is the only one I recognized. We’re getting pictures of it all. It might mean nothing if it was the designs on the artwork. I’ll have to get the exhibit catalog. I didn’t think to match the dirt with the art.”

He sounded annoyed that he hadn’t. He’d just seen what he thought was the devil and panicked and called us. I hoped it wasn’t the devil. I hoped there wasn’t a devil, in general. I was dating a demon, but we didn’t really talk about who he worked for. Mostly Jack just cooked at his restaurant and at home and occasionally helped me kill ghuls.

“No satanic worship?” Lee repeated. “I’m going to find a dead body staked to that table tomorrow?”

Scharf looked at me. I started to nod, but an uncharacteristic caution gripped me. “I have no idea.” I tried to stop talking but couldn’t help myself. “But I don’t think so. I don’t know if this is…”

“If this is your area of expertise?” Scharf prompted.

“Sorry. Like I said, your thief could just be stealing all of this and messing with us by replacing it with dirt. It’s kind of poetic.”

“Sir!”

We turned to see a tall guy in a brown police uniform that looked nothing like the dark blue Metro PD wore.

He waved from the stairs. “Sir, there’s something you should see.”

Lee said, “This is Adelman from park police.”

I frowned. “What park?”

The young man pivoted again, flashing me a gap-toothed smile that made him look younger than me. “The National Mall. America’s front lawn.”

“Cool!” I said when he clearly expected a response. He grinned, and he pivoted to bound up the stairs.

“This museum has its own police?”

Scharf smiled. “All of the Smithsonians.”

We followed him out of the exhibit around an old ship propped up on similar white pedestals. Apparently, the robbers were only interested in arts and crafts. We walked back up wide white stairs to the main foyer of the shuttered museum as Adelman regaled me with the reason he joined the park police and not Metro PD.

“I mean, with the protests, I’m not sorry, you know?”

I nodded vigorously. I’d been way too close to the recent dragon-induced riots that had gripped the city. Not that he knew that part.

When we reached the foyer, I goggled at the three banners dangling from the ceiling: one of the American flag, one with a neon pink leotard whose antecedents weren’t clear, and one with an infinity knot in spun glass stamped with the words special exhibit and St. Patrick’s Day.

“This way,” Adelman gestured us over to an unmarked door painted white to match the walls near the metal detectors and huge glass entry.

Inside, a guy in jeans and a sweatshirt stood at a bank of monitors behind an old-school beige computer with a tricked-out black keyboard, plus a few TV remotes. He looked at us through thick glasses and blinked owlishly. “Uh, just give me a second.”

Adelman was shifting from foot to foot with excitement. His body was concave in his mud-brown uniform. This close, I could see the fuzz of his beard as well. He really might be younger than me.

“What are we going to see?” Lee asked.

“It’s weird,” Adelman said with relish.

“It is?” Scharf repeated with alarm.

“It’s a tape,” Adelman said and flung out a hand. “This is Mr. Turner, one of our digital security experts.”

“And this is my day off,” he offered the room without looking at us. “Don’t know what the hell happened to O’Connor.”

Lee shifted. “O’Connor?”

“O’Connor called out,” Adelman said.

Lee fished out his cell phone. “We have another missing security guard.” Someone mumbled something in the receiver. “No, he called out. He wasn’t on the schedule we used.”

Turner straightened. “You think O’Connor pulled off the biggest art heist in the history of this museum? O’Connor. He could barely find the breakroom.”

“We need to find them, sir,” Lee said in his matter-of-fact voice which meant that this was an emergency. To his phone, he said, “Get the suit on the phone from the main museum office and send squad cars ASAP to his house.”

The guy blinked at us and then went back to the screens, typing away at the computer as cameras speeded up and changed to various exhibits around the museum on the monitors. I saw a military helicopter on a dais, bizarre heavy curtains in the shape of a dress, and others that went by too quickly to see.

“Another security guard?” Scharf asked Lee.

“Yeah, one no-showed, and one called out.”

Turner hit one of the buttons on a remote with the very tip of his index finger, like it was going to shock him or something. Adelman bounced on the balls of his feet and said, “Here’s what you need to see.”

The screens stopped cycling and settled on different views of an intact Irish American Arts and Crafts exhibit. I whistled, impressed despite myself. I’d joked about infinity knots on a jug but most of the stuff on the screen was exquisite. A panel of tiles, green with a gold patina in an intricate pattern dominated the wall opposite the entrance while a multitude of smaller displays held glasswork and ceramic goblets without an obvious Celtic theme. I could see why it was called crafts, not just art. These were practical pieces, meant to be used, not just stuck in a museum.

“What time is this?” Lee asked.

Turner pointed to the running clock in the bottom corner: 3:03 AM.

We watched the silent exhibit for a few more minutes when a figure in black tiptoed into the display carrying a bag, followed by a lot more men in black. I gasped and the assembly looked sharply at me.

“What did you see?” Lee asked.

I frantically scanned the faces of the others: Turner was fidgeting with his phone, but the rest watched without expression. Nobody said, Look at all the people running through the display. I guess we found our thieves.

“Nothing, just, uh, rolled my ankle.” I stood on one leg for a second.

Scharf frowned but the rest went back to the screen.

The room onscreen was crawling with people — short, fair, dark-haired men wearing black clothes and hats and grinning. They were carrying heavy bags into the exhibit, already full, which confused me until I remembered the dirt. It had to get in there somehow.

I tugged on Scharf’s sleeve. “Nothing is happening,” she said to me with a raised eyebrow.

“No shit,” Lee echoed.

The men stopped their dance and I tried to get a quick count; it had to be almost two dozen guys. They stood at attention, each near a different display. A few stuck their hands into their bags.

A hand appeared in view. Its owner stood just under the camera and that’s all I could see of him. Fingers snapped.

The men threw dirt into the air and snatched at the artifacts, one carrying the entire wall of tile by himself. They were far shorter than I was, probably around four feet tall, which meant they were insanely strong or had earth magic like me. They didn’t look like goblins.

Now the room reacted with gasps and shouts.

“Play it back!” Lee demanded.

ABOUT THE AUTHOR

Kyler Anne Ford writes brainy, funny, feminist, and diverse speculative fiction that explores what it means to be human, the old myths we tell ourselves to help us sleep at night, and our dreams of what’s next. Learn more at kyleranneford.com.
